                                     EXERCISE
                              Questions on supervision
   1.	 How do you evaluate and monitor your practice?
   2.	 What do you need in your work in order to be effective? And what happens
       when this is lacking?
   3.	 Describe a good supervisory experience. What were its characteristics?
   4.	 Describe a bad supervisory experience. What were its characteristics?
   5.	 What do you know about how you learn?



It is likely that supervision will be different during training and the early post-
qualification years than after the therapist has matured and gained a lot of profes-
sional experience. Seasoned therapists may prefer to have supervision in a peer
relationship, either in one-to-one or in a peer group or both. At the beginning of
the therapist’s career the supervisor will usually and necessarily have a greater level
of experience. As the existential counsellor or therapist gains experience and resil-
ience she or he will gain enormously from choosing someone from outside the
existential tribe, as long as that person is sympathetic to existential ideas and ways of
working. This will contribute a challenging additional perspective and will include
aspects such as a different understanding of the client’s past, internal world and rela-
tionships, including the therapeutic relationship. This will enable the therapist to
broaden her or his knowledge base at the same time as gaining a deeper understand-
ing of the existential perspective and its connections with other perspectives. We
have nothing to fear from other therapeutic perspectives and can, on the contrary,
learn much from them. It is also good practice to get a different experience of super-
vision periodically. A supervisory relationship that has become too comfortable
contradicts the two principles of ‘super’ and ‘vision’ mentioned above. Having said
that, it is perfectly possible for a good supervisor to maintain a fresh outlook and not
settle for a merely cosy, comforting supervisory relationship, even after many years of
working with a particular supervisee.
   The issue of responsibility is central in both existential philosophy and existential
supervision. An important question is that of the extent of the supervisor’s responsibil-
ity, especially about the nature of this responsibility. How can we exercise such respon-
sibility for the therapist’s work, while simultaneously acknowledging our own
autonomy and that of the supervisee and the client?
   We need to remember that:

   supervision can only ever be about the last session and not about the next session;
   the supervisor should not tell the supervisee what to do next;
   for the client’s sake the supervisor needs to believe in the supervisee’s ability to
     practise professionally – though obviously if the supervisor has doubts about this
     it needs to be addressed;

                                        • 173 •
       • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


   the focus should always be on the supervisee’s work with clients – which of
     course does not exclude the supervisee’s own experience or the relationship with
     the supervisor.

Having said that, there are differences that revolve around the issues of freedom and
responsibility. The main one is that the supervisor has to reserve the right to take on
different roles where necessary. Supervisors must:

   Be supportive of the supervisee’s work. They must be alert to any gaps in the
     supervisee’s factual knowledge and decide how these gaps may be filled. It can
     be very tempting for the inexperienced supervisor to try to impress the coun-
     sellor with their knowledge and to explain too much. This sort of explanation
     is invariably given to relieve the supervisor’s anxiety or to impress the supervi-
     see with the supervisor’s learning and will inevitably lead to a reduction in
     collaborative dialogue.
   Be challenging of the supervisee’s work. Nevertheless, it will often be apparent
     that the therapist is unaware of how she or he is, for example, contributing
     to a problematic situation which she or he is attributing to the client or the
     supervisee. It is the supervisor’s responsibility to both therapist and client to
     address this. Occasionally it may be useful to speculate about other ways of
     being that might be available to the client of which the therapist is unaware.
     This will, however, always be in order to return to the main idea, which is to
     help the client to learn to be attentive to who they are and learn to just be
     themselves.
   Be aware of any lapses in ethical standards and decide how to meet these.

These roles will be modified in different ways if the supervisor is paid by an agency
to supervise the counsellors in that agency.
   Existential supervision will look at how the client’s way of being in the four dimen-
sions is translated into the issues they are preoccupied with and tries to understand
and make sense of these. It will encourage the therapist to put themselves in the cli-
ent’s place and feel into the client’s world, to look at the therapist’s understanding of
what the client says, does and experiences and look for connections between their
lives, both in terms of similarities and differences.


Note-taking and presenting work
in supervision

Existentially the therapist needs to find a way to reflect on their experience with their
client while at the same time embracing the aliveness of the encounter. Since the
supervisor will never be present at the actual time of the session we need to find some
way to make a record of the session that we can use later, in order to reflect on it and
learn from it.


                                        • 174 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


   In presenting work in supervision, given that we cannot have access to the session
itself, what the counsellor needs to cultivate is a way of recalling the spirit of the
session, the thoughts, feelings and dialogue that when presented in supervision can
offer the possibility of an embodied recollection of the experience of the session,
rather than an intellectualized and sanitized version of it. There is a danger in any
reflection process, and supervision is primarily about reflection, of losing track of
the unfolding relationship between the client and the therapist. Consequently we
need a way of presenting in supervision that reduces the possibility of objectifica-
tion; when the therapy is seen as a technical rather than a human intervention, and
also the possibility of subjectification; when the client becomes a figment of the
supervisee’s imagination and appears within supervision as a collection of memories,
desires and impressions which are hard to separate from the supervisee’s own
un-bracketed assumptions. To untangle these, supervision will sometimes need to
concentrate on the therapeutic relationship. At other times it may need to focus on
the supervisee’s own related issues.



           MAKING A RECORD OF YOUR SESSIONS FOR
                    USE IN SUPERVISION
  Here is a formal framework you can use to record your sessions. It is important
  to make these notes as soon as possible after the session as the accuracy and
  detail of your memory will diminish very quickly and be replaced by assumptions
  and constructions. If these dimensions are recorded then there is more likeli-
  hood that the spirit of the session can be reconstructed faithfully afterwards.

                                              Client

     The client was mainly preoccupied with …...........................................
     The obstacles and dilemmas she was facing in that process were ….....
     Her ultimate purpose seems to be ….......................................................
     She is undermining this purpose by doing ….........................................
     What she wants to do differently or understand better is …....................


                                            Therapist

     I did ……….	(e.g. what you said, how you acted, what your timekeeping
                 was like).
     I thought ….	(e.g. what thoughts occurred to you before, during and after
                   the session about what how the session went).

                                                                                    (Continued)




                                             • 175 •
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •



   (Continued)

     I felt……….	(e.g. what feelings came up for you before, during and after
                 the session).
     I learnt……	(e.g. what you learnt about your client, your relationship
                 with your client and your practice that you didn’t know
                 before, and what you might do differently).



It is compulsory in some work settings to make notes for the use of the work set-
ting. If this is the case then the workplace supervisor will give guidelines of what
they want. It is a professional requirement for therapists to have proper records of
their work. If the therapist is required to give evidence in a legal context, not hav-
ing notes will be considered to be a sign of lack of professionalism. It is therefore
good practice to summarize your experience of each session in writing. Also it is
difficult to use supervision effectively without notes. Our memories are not that
good or reliable. Notes can be about thoughts, feelings, questions, impressions,
hunches, the client’s purpose and problems, but may also involve small excerpts of
dialogue.
   Notes should be made as soon as possible after the session because impressions and
feelings will be forgotten first, then thoughts and finally actions. It is good practice to
make your own notes, which are different from and in addition to those required by
your workplace; they are more of a record of your personal experience of the sessions
and a record of your professional learning.
   If you work with 50-minute sessions, on the hour, that leaves you 10 minutes to
write your notes and reflect on your work. If 10 minutes is not enough, you can fill
your notes out later. Jotting down what you want to remember of a session makes it
easier to move on to the next client. You will feel free to let go of the client’s issues,
knowing that you have made a record of them. Having the notes reinforces the expe-
rience of the session and makes it more likely to be recalled in the next session. It is
impossible to recall an entire session, so what results will inevitably be selective; eve-
rything is subject to inadvertent omissions and revision. But accurate record keeping
is a skill that can be learnt and will improve as experience accumulates.
   The act of writing notes is different from the act of reading notes. It is often enough
just to write them. Reading the notes of the previous session immediately before the
next session can sometimes be useful if we need to recall a detail, but it can also pre-
vent the therapist from being with the client in the present, concentrating too much
on the previous week.
   As already suggested earlier, you may at some point be required to testify about
what happened in a piece of work with a client and keeping notes will be invaluable
and help you to be realistic about your work. Some existential supervisors advocate
the use of verbatim records, but like all ways of presenting in supervision, these have
advantages and disadvantages.
   Another way to do this is making an audio or video recording of the session, but
this is rarely possible for both ethical and practical reasons. Some clients like to record

                                        • 176 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


sessions for their own sake and if you do not mind this happening, it may help them
benefit doubly from the work. It may be helpful to ask them to bring their reflections
about the work to the next session if they are doing this.



   KEY POINTS

   •• Existential supervision starts from the experience of the therapeutic relationship.
   •• The existential supervisor will always acknowledge the autonomy of the
      therapist while at the same time taking responsibility for her or his own
      autonomy and knowledge.
   •• Existential supervision is a collaborative search for the truth of human existence.
   •• Making notes is a part of being an effective and professional practitioner.


COACHING

Although many counsellors may be new to coaching, coaching itself is not new, being
established for many years in sport and business. There are many points of contact
between coaching and counselling and particularly so in the skills that are needed.
Because coaching has been a very flexible discipline it has been able to draw from
many fields. Existential coaching has added a new and important dimension to this
developing field (Deurzen and Hanaway, 2012).
   One of the differences between psychotherapy and counselling is that psycho-
therapy arose within the medical model and therefore has assumptions of pathology,
dysfunction and cure embedded in it. Counselling arose within an educational context
and in some ways as a reaction against the prevailing medical model in mental health,
so counselling has contrasting assumptions of health, well-being, personal effectiveness
and of life as a process of continuous development. Although coaching developed
independently it has much in common with the assumptions of counselling and it is
eminently compatible with an existential approach.
   Many counsellors may think of coaching as sophisticated advice giving, but this is
to misunderstand it. Although in its narrowest form coaching can be seen as focusing
on problem solving, of being concerned with the ‘doing’ aspects of existence, good
coaching is more than this: it is learning to learn, rather than teaching. Like counselling,
the effective coach will need to have struggled in the areas she or he is coaching in.
   We have said earlier that counselling practice based on phenomenology will be
closer to the truths of human existence. This means that since phenomenology is a
research method, as long as the ethics of the particular work environment are consist-
ent with the ethical principles of phenomenology there will not be a problem.
   What an existential approach can bring to coaching practice is that of emphasizing
the ‘being’ aspects of existence. Experiences like those of uncertainty, the frustrated
search for meaning and purpose, or for freedom and responsibility and the inevitable
struggle with paradox and dilemma will be at the centre of existential coaching, thus
shedding a different light on the goal of problem solving.

                                         • 177 •
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


   Existential coaching will enable a person to explore different aspects of exist-
ence, so as to understand them better. For instance, they may come to realize that
two ways of experience that are often seen as opposed, that is, doing and being, in
actual fact complement each other. Existential coaching will rely on the practice
of phenomenology and will avoid unethical practices. Existential coaches will not
fall for the trap of suggesting behaviours to their clients, but will stick with the
phenomenological exploration of their experience so as to allow a widening of the
focus of coaching from simple learning techniques to understanding life and a
person’s place in it. Coaching can be seen as a form of life supervision in which
mastery is achieved not simply by learning new tools and techniques to control
and fix problems but by engaging with mystery and expanding our range of human
experience.
   Every setting brings a new set of constraints and opportunities, and coaching is no
exception. One of the opportunities coaching brings is to reconsider the rationale
behind some counselling traditions, like session times, frequency and length of con-
tract. Because the work environment is different, different values and expectations
apply. There is a tradition within counselling of short-term contracts, particularly
within the NHS and in Employee Assistance Programmes, and most therapeutic
approaches including the existential approach have devised short-term models. The
same is true in coaching. Victor Frankl’s (2000) logotherapy was an early version of
short-term existential coaching, and Strasser and Strasser’s (1997) is a more contem-
porary model. Psychotherapy has, however, been much more reluctant to reconsider
the issue of length and frequency of sessions. Within coaching, while the work is
invariably time limited, the sessions are not necessarily 50 minutes long, nor weekly.
The length and frequency will be determined by many issues such as availability of
the coach, the needs and availability of the client and the resources of the funding
body. Consequently, the contract for example may be for six 2-hour sessions spread
unevenly over a period of 6 months. Another difference is that the coaching may well
take place in the workplace, and one reason this is possible is that it is seen as a
legitimate non-pathologizing part of work life. It is about maximizing performance
and potential in a professional setting, rather than targeting psychological problems. It
is not evidence of a deficiency or a punishment for poor performance. Existential
coaching, with its emphasis on ethics and philosophical understanding, fits right into
this model.
   The coach will spend some time at the beginning of the work tuning into the
client’s worldview by use of the skills of phenomenology. At the same time the coach
will be monitoring the relationship to see how the client is making use of the
coaching and will also be formulating and reformulating the issue as the client is
explaining it and focusing it into discrete goals which will be checked with the cli-
ent. This calls for a great deal of skill and humility. On occasions it may be necessary
to reiterate and explain the purpose and different responsibilities of the coach and
the client. Bearing in mind the focused short-term nature of the work, it will often
be necessary to introduce information or facts that the client does not know but
needs to know. In this situation being a coach is a bit like being a supervisor. Also
because of its focused fixed-term nature, coaching will typically employ a number


                                       • 178 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


of techniques and strategies. One of these is the use of psychometric tests like the
Myers Briggs (MBTI). Used phenomenologically and existentially, this has the pur-
pose of putting the client in touch with forgotten life projects. It is like a snapshot
of what is and can be a way for the client to engage with denied aspects of their
freedom. In a similar way techniques like guided fantasy, collaborative goal setting,
self-help including homework, mindfulness and focusing can be used and taught.
This can obviously endanger the existential phenomenological nature of the work,
but this is always retrieved because of the meaning focus of existential coaching. The
client is enabled to consider their life’s purpose, or lack of it, and to become much
more aware of assumptions and values.
   In common with all short-term counselling models, the work of the coach tends
to be less aimed at looking at psychological problems as such and more aimed towards
action, goal directedness and structured learning.Taking a phenomenological approach
can allow the coach to focus on goals at the same time as offering a space for the cli-
ent to slow down and take time to reconsider aims and objectives, decisions and
strategies. It can be a space for both coach and client to use their imagination. While
this may be more familiar for the counsellor who does coaching, a phenomenological
approach might not be familiar to the ambitious business client who is more accus-
tomed to techniques and solutions. Therefore the coach may need to explain the
rationale behind their approach. This is not that there is no structure to the session but
that the structure will allow for a focus on values and meaning and lead to a different
quality of decision making and goal setting.



                                     EXERCISE
                     Questions on your effectiveness at work
   1.	   Think of a past work situation that was causing you some problems.
   2.	   What was your part in the maintenance of the problems?
   3.	   Why did it take you so long to do something about it?
   4.	   How did you solve it?
   5.	   In hindsight, what other solutions may there have been?



There are no issues that do not have an existential dimension and all work-setting
issues resonate in all the four worlds.
   In the physical world, a client unfulfilled in his job is not just thinking about his
job, he is facing a life-changing event – reconsidering what he wants to do for the rest
of his working life. This can impact on the ethical world in terms of the need to do
something in accordance with one’s values, but in itself it creates an important issue
to explore: how will my concrete physical existence alter if I am to make the changes
envisaged?
   In the social world, we all have to work with and to get on with other people –
this is an existential given, and one of the givens of corporate work is the potential


                                        • 179 •
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


conflict between the needs of the organisation and the needs of the individual.
One manifestation of this will be that it is frequently the company who pays for
the coaching. This means that the company will have a vested interest in the out-
come of the work that may be at odds with what the client wishes to get from it.
The coach may also feel this dilemma, but it is one that can rarely be solved by
being avoided or with an either/or solution. It has to be identified, faced head on
and opened out.
   Psychologically, a common issue is that of managing leadership. We all have
different qualities and the corporate world invests certain people with different
amounts of authority depending on their role in the organisation. An existential
perspective on this would not simply be framed in terms of the corporate struc-
ture and responsibilities, but in terms of our need to cooperate and the different
needs of the leader and the led and of everyone’s interlocking and co-constituted
identities within the microcosm of the company. The individual has certain
desires, talents, qualities and interests that fit more or less well with the company’s
requirements. The interlocking microcosm of the company and the macrocosm of
the wider world form the backdrop to the way in which each individual fits into
the niche they have made or earned in their department. Dissonance in this
respect is rife. Conflict is common and moreover is expensive and not just in
financial terms. Employees who are discontent are not only inefficient, but they
create a further atmosphere of dissatisfaction around them. It is a paradox that
conflict is contact of a sort that both expresses and masks the need for contact of
a different, more understanding sort. Disputes in a culture where winning and
beating is the norm can cause problems for all and calls for a different approach
to conflict resolution, where people can learn to examine and respect different
points of view. This involves both parties in the dispute winning something new,
for instance the sense of being seen, respected and understood, rather than one
party winning and the other being beaten. In the long term, the model of com-
petition is destructive to organizations as well as to people. Existential coaches
can teach alternative conflict resolution models and generate a new atmosphere
of respect for individuals in an organization.
   It is well known that people work more effectively when they are able to act in
accordance with their chosen ethical set of values and when these are reflected in the
values of the workplace. When this occurs the employee will be more able to own
their actions and those of the organization with pride, rather than with shame or
embarrassment. Existential coaching will encourage the client to examine their cho-
sen ethical values and consider the extent to which it is meeting their needs and
whether it is echoed by the workplace, to consider what active changes they may wish
to make as a result.
   Companies that are undergoing organizational change will frequently encoun-
ter conflict and stress in their staff. This can be seen existentially as a natural
human response to change, powerlessness, absurdity and uncertainty. It will not
help the transition to label the problems as caused by a difficult and resistant staff
group or trade union. If brought in early enough in the process, an existentially
informed coach can bring a new and more cooperative and creative perspective
on organizational change.

                                       • 180 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •



   KEY POINTS

   •• Coaching is about learning to learn.
   •• Coaching work has a more flexible stance towards session regularity and
      contract length.
   •• Existential principles are always present in work-related issues.
   •• An employee who is able to work according to her or his ethical principles
      and with autonomy will be both happier and more productive



WORKING WITH COUPLES AND FAMILY
RELATIONSHIPS

We have talked earlier about the principle of being-in-the-world that underpins all
existential philosophy and practice. This means that relationships, or rather relational-
ity, is central to existence and in this sense individuality is secondary to relationship
and all our work is done against the backdrop of our relationships with features in the
physical, the social, the personal and the spiritual worlds.
   However, it is only recently that the issue of how to work existentially with
relationships has been written about (Tantam and Deurzen, 2005; Deurzen and
Iacovou, 2013).
   Although the main existential philosophers have looked at different aspects of rela-
tionships in different ways they are all in agreement on the following:

   Our lives are embedded in relationships to others and that human life finds its
     meaning in relationships. (Buber)
   In order for our relationships to be loving and respectful of the other we have to
     be able to love ourselves and our lives first rather than expecting others to take
     responsibility. (Kierkegaard and Nietzsche)
   There will always be a tension between our need for individuality and our need
     to be a part of a whole. Between the need to join, to fuse, with another in order
     not to feel alone, and the need to separate from others in order to feel individual.
     (Heidegger and Sartre)
   The problem of maintaining a secure yet fluid sense of self within an intimate
     relationship will only be solved by embracing the existential reality that other
     people have their own autonomy and it is only the awareness of our difference
     that can help us to understand and to respect each other. This is the meaning of
     love. (Buber, Levinas and Sartre)
   While being a valuable quality, a life built round self-sufficiency is an impover-
     ished life. Without honest and reliable feedback from others we are able to trust,
     we are less than human. (Jaspers)

                                        • 181 •
         • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


In working with relationships an issue that frequently arises is that of sex and sexuality.
It is often the issue that brings people to counselling in the first place.
   Of the philosophers, Merleau-Ponty (1962) has said the most about sexuality. His
point is that, rather than separating sexuality off as a separate part of existence, we should
instead recognise the way it is present in all our relations with others. It is an inseparable
part of our being-in-the-world. We can think of the sexual act as being the aspect of
sexuality that is driven by desire, but sexuality is more than just sexual acts. We are per-
manently and unavoidably sexual beings. Although sexuality is ontological, the meanings
we take from it are ontic. As Simone de Beauvoir (1973) said,‘One is not born, but rather
becomes, a woman.’ The same could be said of being a man, or anybody who questions
the meaning of their gender. Sexuality unites our physical, social, personal and spiritual
worlds and enables us to be truly embodied. By thinking of it in this way we move
everyday notions of sex and sexuality towards an inevitable and de-pathologized sexual
element in all encounters. It is remarkable that something that is so much a part of every
one of us can cause so many problems. There is a strong sedimented social and cultural
effect here and much talk, and indeed much of the language of sexuality is couched in
heterosexist male terms. A simple example of this is that heterosexual intercourse is talked
about in terms of the erect penis penetrating rather than being engulfed by the vagina.
   The extreme sedimentation of beliefs about sex and sexuality means that therapists
have a responsibility to examine their own assumptions about what constitutes a
‘normal’ sexual relationship and what part sex and sexuality has in their lives. Without
this therapists may either impose their own assumptions on the client or be unable
to understand the client and their relationships.



                                       EXERCISE
                         Questions on sex and relationships
   1.	 Describe your idea of a normal couple relationship.
   2.	 What is your view on pornography?
   3.	 What qualities do your relationships with friends tend to have?
   4.	 What qualities do your relationship with sexual partners tend to have?
   5.	 What are the differences between the friendships you have with men and
       with women?
   6.	 How do you know you are a man (or a woman)?



Since sexuality is a part of existence it follows that we try not to think of sex in sim-
ply mechanistic terms, but the ways in which sexual phenomena may relate to broader
aspects of a person’s life (Barker, 2012; Richards and Barker, 2013; Langdridge and
Barker, 2008). For example, a difficulty in reaching orgasm may relate to an inability
to let go and be vulnerable, and treating it as a mechanical dysfunction without under-
standing its meaning may be harmful. Nevertheless, although it is important for pos-
sible organic causes to be ruled out prior to counselling, we must not forget that even
such organic causes will always have an existential meaning.

                                          • 182 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


   Relationships are sustained by love and many people who come to couple counsel-
ling think they are good at giving love but that others, usually their partners, are not
good at loving in return. This sees love as a commodity that can be passed between or
done to people. Existentially love is not a commodity, and seeing love and sex in this
way will inevitably lead to a sense of personal alienation and a consequence is the com-
modification and technologizing of the body and of sexuality and its removal from the
experience of love. Nevertheless it is in feeling able to love and be loved that people
thrive. As well as confusing love and sex, people often confuse love and need, love and
like, love and friendship, love and lust and love and control. Existential therapy will
address such issues openly and enable people to examine the way in which they use
others in an instrumental way or allow themselves to be so used. Of course this may also
bring up more disturbing issues of abusing others or allowing oneself to be abused.
   Love is connection and respect and when couples come to counselling they are
trusting us with their connections to others, therefore there is a primary need for the
counsellor to be sensitive, open and even-handed. As with working with individuals,
we do not see the work as primarily about problem solving. Existentially there is a
commitment to liberation and autonomy rather than to particular predetermined
concrete outcomes.
   Overall we can think of the aims of existential relationship counselling as being to:

   Create a climate in which the couple feels able to explore their way of being
     individually and together that can be witnessed by the other. We do this by being
     phenomenological and genuinely interested in the world that the two people
     have created together.
   Encourage each person to note how they impact on the other. We may say some-
     thing like, ‘John, did you hear what Jane just said? How was it for you when she
     said that? What did you make of it?’
   Recognize the otherness of the other through true dialogue with the other. We
     may say something like, ‘What would you like Jane to know that you don’t think
     she knows?’ taking care to discuss this and also giving John the same opportunity
     in return.
   Uncover entrenched worldviews that block understanding. We do this by phenom-
     enologically exploring, clarifying and challenging the meanings that emerge in the
     dialogue. We can also do it by working with and through the body in a way more
     familiar to Gestalt practitioners, for example through sculpting. In this way the client
     will better understand the emotional quality of their relational dynamics by physi-
     cally enacting aspects of their experience.We also do this by exploring the points of
     similarity and difference in the couple’s value systems using our own knowledge of
     which sort of differences are likely to be insurmountable and which not.
   Assist the development of the individual’s autonomy and ability to take respon-
     sibility for their actions. We do this by asking the couple to own their thoughts
     and feelings and not to blame or attribute them to the other. We may say
     something like, ‘John, can you repeat how that was for you without seeking to
     blame or point fingers?’

                                          • 183 •
       • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


   Help the individuals to understand that conflict in relationships is inevitable and
     that it is important to meet it in a calm and robust manner rather than to evade,
     deny or despair at it. Many couples are afraid of the destructiveness of anxiety,
     anger and resentment. Existential relationship therapists will encourage conflict
     and difference and show couples how to express it openly without reproach or
     judgment. This allows partners to learn that it can be managed and understood,
     and this immediately leads to greater trust and more intimacy.

Since the existential relationship counsellor is guided by the same principles of phe-
nomenology and a commitment to the client’s autonomy that inform individual work,
she will feel freer to decide on the basis of the clients’ needs whether they need to be
seen individually or together, in what proportion and with what frequency. Whether
seen individually or together, the focus of the work should always be their relationship.
   Many couples seek counselling because of the existence or threat of violence. In
these cases when love gets confused with control, counsellors need to assess the likely
risks of such work. It is particularly important in these cases to ensure that both part-
ners make informed decisions to participate.The risk to client safety is also rarely static
and is likely to increase if feelings of fear, vulnerability and lack of control become
unmanageable (Adams and Jepson, 2013).
   It is important to note that suspicions about love, respect, and being listened to and
being understood are almost always in the background in couple work. Counsellors
and therapists need to show that these things can be taken seriously and are important.
They need to ensure that they are even-handed and have no favouritism. Issues like
the age and gender of the counsellor, which one of the couple is most committed to
the counselling and which person’s view is perceived to be preferred by the counsel-
lor need to be addressed openly so as to stop them interfering with the trust the
couple can put in both counsellor and the work itself. It is helpful to remember that
having two different perspectives represented in the room is an asset from a phenom-
enological perspective. We can all learn from seeing ourselves as another, especially as
someone who knows us well perceives us.
   When seeing couples individually another point to be aware of is that of confiden-
tiality. It is almost inevitable that one of the couple will say something individually
that they have not said or would be wary of saying with their partner present. This is,
after all, a part of the issue that led them to be in counselling in the first place. How
this is dealt with will vary depending on the context and the individuals involved.
Such tensions of confidentiality and secretiveness give us something to work with and
towards. Ideally in the long run such gaps of confidentiality and trust will be bridged
as partners create greater closeness, safety and resilience with each other. It is not the
counsellor’s responsibility to open up issues, though it is for the counsellor to help
create an atmosphere that is safe and robust enough to make it possible for partners
to face up to such issues of their own accord.
   One of us (EvD) has developed a way of working with couples where individual
work is carried out with one partner in front of the other partner, so that each learns
to see the other in all their intimate fears, worries, longings and desires, and also learn
to listen to each other before they learn, perhaps for the first time, to be in dialogue
(Deurzen, 2012).

                                        • 184 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


   The existential and phenomenological study of families was pioneered by Laing
and Esterson (Laing, 1960; Laing and Esterson, 1964; Esterson, 1970) and the princi-
ples of working with families are very similar to those of working with couples. One
big difference between couples and families is that, with the exception of some
arranged marriages, couples choose in adulthood to be with each other. Being in a
couple is the product of a mutual active choice. Being a member of a family is of a
different order. We do not choose the families we are born into and we do not choose
our fathers, mothers, sons and daughters.



                                     EXERCISE
                                Questions on families
   1.	   What was the stated or unstated motto of your family of origin?
   2.	   Describe your position in your family or origin.
   3.	   Describe a typical family mealtime.
   4.	   How did your family of origin express feelings of love or hate?



Heidegger talks about the facts of our existence that are imposed upon us without
any choice, like our family gender and culture, and that we are thrown into these.
Such features can never change, though they can take on different meanings to us
as we get older. Alongside these are those circumstances that arise out of a per-
sonal action. He described these as circumstances we ‘fall’ into, and we can change
these when we understand our responsibility for them. Every family is both
unique and similar, and understanding how families work is necessary for existen-
tial counsellors because the task of understanding your place in your family of
origin and to authentically transcend it by owning it rather than inauthentically
disowning it is at the core of any existential search for self knowledge (Stadlen
and Stadlen, 2005).
   Most clients are implicitly or explicitly trying to understand their past and present
family relationships and the existential therapist needs to understand not just how
theirs worked but also how they have managed or may manage to transcend it.



  KEY POINTS

  •• We are fundamentally relational.
  •• Our sexuality is present in some way in all our relationships.
  •• Love is connection and respect, but can often be confused with need, control,
     liking, friendship and lust.
  •• A counsellor cannot understand couples, relationships and families until
     they understand their own.


                                       • 185 •
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


WORKING WITH GROUPS

The interest and concern that existential philosophers have with respect to groups mir-
rors the stance of wider society. This is to say that while acknowledging that we live in
a world of others, philosophers (e.g. Kierkegaard and Nietzsche) have been extremely
wary of the power of groups. Kierkegaard said that ‘wherever there is a crowd there is
untruth’ (2005: dedication) and Nietzsche talked about the ‘obligation to lie according
to the fixed convention [of the] herd’ (1982: 46). Heidegger was only slightly less suspi-
cious when he talked of the anaesthetising effect of the ‘they’ (das Man) to divert the
individual from their personal commitment to discovering the nature of Being. He calls
this ‘averageness’ or ‘everydayness’ (Heidegger, 1962: 164) and says that falling into and
accepting this takes away the anxiety of choice and responsibility by allowing the per-
son to defer to others and to convention when asking personal questions about how to
live. There is also a tendency, as Sartre said, to find societally approved predetermined
roles to play in order to evade and deny our freedom. There is, of course, an emotional
and existential payoff for this, and it is that one is relieved of major worries and ques-
tions about how to be. But there is also an existential consequence, that the person will
be made aware of their inauthenticity or bad faith by feelings of existential guilt expe-
rienced as depression or anxiety of one sort or another.
   This suspicion that the group cannot help but override the needs of the individual
has been present through the ages and is echoed in contemporary political and cul-
tural discourse. We are both attracted to and fear groups. They encapsulate our great-
est fears of annihilation at the same time as our hopes of belonging. Attempting to
solve the dilemma of how to be an individual in a world of others either by taking an
oppositional stance to social conventions and institutions or by going along them
uncritically can never be a long-term solution. The only solution can be one that
accommodates both and makes them personal.
   Of the philosophers, Sartre has said more than most about groups and he started
from the observation that groups are a part of everyday life and that we have an inbuilt
ability to recognise the degree of groupness, or cohesiveness, of a group. This sense of
cohesiveness is a consequence of the recognition of a common task or purpose and
leads to the desire to maintain the group relationship by what he called ‘reciprocity’.
In this way group identity is formed. Once a group has formed out of common pur-
pose its next task is for the individuals to work out how to be with each other, and
each member of the group has the freedom and responsibility to decide whether to
be controlled by the group or try to control the group. While many will try to dom-
inate, others will offer themselves to be dominated. Others will find the situation so
difficult that they will try to withdraw from it all together. Eventually, if the group
finds a way to affirm its common purpose with reciprocity and respect, the members
of the group may discover the possibility of collaboration, where each is respectful of
self and others and all benefit. The existential facilitator will aim to actively propagate
this situation of learning and communicative cooperation.
   Whether we are aware of it or not, we are all members of different sociocultural
groups that define who we are; these include peer groups, occupational groups,
religious entities, societies and so on. Each of these has their own particular systems
of meaning, modes of interacting, possibilities, constraints and limitations. In our
                                        • 186 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


everydayness, we act in accordance with these identities, which partially have been
foisted upon us and partially we have created.
   An existential view of groups sees the individual as always being part of a network
and this idea underpins the phenomenological basis of group analytic practice, which
was developed by S.H. Foulkes (Tantam and Deurzen, 2005) who, in turn, was influ-
enced by Husserl, Heidegger and Merleau-Ponty. Foulkes saw psychological distur-
bance as essentially a disturbance of communication, as a kind of individualism that
rejects contact with others. In this principle there is some overlap with the work of
Laing and Esterson (1964). The group is the place where this disturbance of commu-
nication and relatedness can be seen to be enacted, not simply talked about. It can
therefore be worked on more directly. In this sense the real therapeutic work does not
just go on within people but between people.



                                      EXERCISE
                                 Questions on groups
   1.	   What groups are you a member of and what are their values?
   2.	   Do you regard yourself as a ‘team player’?
   3.	   Do your friends know each other?
   4.	   What role(s) do you tend to take in groups?
   5.	   How do you use social media?
   6.	   What is your ideal social, or work, environment?



Therapeutic groups, including the self-development groups that are a part of thera-
peutic training courses, all share the purpose and value of reflecting on and sharing
emotional experiences and reactions in order that personal learning may occur. That
groups are universally problematic is demonstrated by how common the fear is of
being overwhelmed by the emotional experience of the other members of such
groups. The resolution of this problem is to find a way to be with this fear such that
it is understood, tolerated and even savoured.
    The role of the group facilitator in these sorts of groups is critical because she or he
has the responsibility to establish reciprocal relations with the group members, or in
other words to provide a model of how people will be treated. Existentially, effective
leadership comes about if the leader can act with the values of respect for individual
freedom and autonomy, for diversity and for honesty rather than deception and domi-
nance. The group leader must constantly monitor the level of fear and anxiety in the
group and try to maintain it at optimum level for the group. Too little anxiety and the
group will becoming boring, avoidant and may even grind to a halt. Too much anxiety
and members will attack, withdraw, hide, or freeze. Reciprocity will be impossible if there
is either too much or too little tension. Although the leader may model reciprocity, the
task of achieving group reciprocity is more difficult to achieve.This is because the ability
to have and maintain a dialogue that respects emotional, social and existential experi-
ences of difference is related to the ability of the members individually and collectively

                                         • 187 •
         • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


to maintain a both/and stance to the basic dilemmas of being in a group. The facilitator
will need to directly address people’s ambivalence to groups for this reason.
   Across all therapeutic modalities the history of therapeutic groups has shown that
pragmatic considerations, the need to provide value for money has often been the
driver for understanding how therapeutic groups operate and how they work best.
   There are two main ways of working with groups. One is to regard the group itself
as the object of study and therapeutic change. This corresponds to the group analytic
approach. The group conductor regards him or herself as a part of the group but directs
all comments to the group as a whole rather than to individual members, and as in
individual therapy is both outside the group but also involved in the group. The group
conductor will endeavour not to engage in conversations with individual group mem-
bers as this can be divisive and hinder the group process. There is only one group task
and that is for the individuals to find a lived solution to the dilemma of how to be an
individual in a world of others and how to communicate honestly and be open to them.
This method places equal responsibility on each group member with the faith that such
a thing is possible and that the different ways individuals do this will constitute the group
process. This is the work of the group. The conductor’s job is to point out when and
how this is not being done.
   The other way of working with groups is for the leader to be rather more active
and focus on individuals and their relationships to each other and to comment on
these, rather than on the process of the group as a whole.The objective of such groups
is often more for individuals to work out their personal issues as they experience these
in relation to other people and get honest feedback on them. Groups run in this way
tend to have more explicit tasks, like managing depression or addiction. While these
are the two alternatives, many groups are run in a way that combines the two when
necessary. This is shown well in Yalom’s The Schopenhauer Cure (2007), which describes
a more existential model of groupwork.
   Yalom (2007) has reviewed the research and practice on group psychotherapy and
arrives at the following findings:

1.	 Leaders have four functions:
    ••    Emotional stimulation – challenging.
    ••    Caring – support and encouragement.
    ••    Meaning attribution – explaining and interpreting.
    ••    Executive needs – maintaining boundaries and rules.
    ••    Of these, a high degree of caring and meaning attribution, and a moderate
          degree of emotional stimulation and boundary keeping are correlated with a
          good outcome.
2.	 Heterogeneous groups tend to work less well than homogeneous groups because
    there is less opportunity to develop a sense of cohesiveness. The homogeneity of
    addictions anonymous groups is a large contributor to their effectiveness.
3.	 Generally groups will work better for people with interpersonal, relationship
    issues.

                                         • 188 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


4.	 Because the setting of a group is social, there is a large pull on the facilitator to
    make interpretations or give explanations about participants’ motivations.
    Consequently they are less likely to be effective. Instead the phenomenological
    group therapist will confine themselves to:
    •• describing what the participant’s behaviour looks like;
    •• describing what feelings the participant’s behaviour generates;
    •• describing how the participant’s behaviour is impacting on the group and on
       other people;
    •• making clear how this is affecting the way in which the participant is being
       seen and related to by others;
    •• describing how the participant’s behaviour influences the opinion each per-
       son has of themselves;
    •• describing how individuals are contributing and being generous towards the
       group or other members;
    •• describing the purpose and values held by group members, or pointing out
       where these may clash or be unclear or undecided;
    •• describing how existential challenges are being handled or avoided.



   KEY POINTS

   •• Our attitude to groups is one of ambivalence.
   •• The existential group leader or facilitator should model openness and
      respect, while pointing out how the existential givens are being evaded and
      denied.
   •• Most existential group leaders tend to combine the group analytic and the
      individual approach.
   •• In line with phenomenological practice, the group leader’s interventions
      will generally be descriptive and purposeful in exploring meanings, rather
      than interpretive.



WORKING WITH PEOPLE AT EITHER END
OF THE LIFE SPAN

In counselling training, the tradition has developed that working with children and
young people requires specialist knowledge and skills that are in addition to or differ-
ent from those needed to work with adults, and that this need is met by specialist
courses. This in turn is reflected in the lack of emphasis on human development
throughout the life span on training courses and when it is present it is always through
the lens of the therapeutic perspective of the course. For example, there is a great deal
on child development on psychoanalytically orientated courses, but little on others.

                                        • 189 •
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


   Consequently most counsellors feel ill equipped to work with children and may
claim not to know anything about children, in spite of having been a child themselves
and therefore having had direct experience of childhood, and in many cases being
parents themselves. One can speculate on what it is that has conspired to make many
adults lose touch with their own experience and knowledge of childhood, but how-
ever it happens the temptation to standardize and objectify children by age, maturity,
academic ability, medical diagnosis, behaviour and so on is very strong. Existentially, a
person does not develop by moving sequentially from one stage of development to
the next. This is a natural scientific, biological view which encourages a normative
view of human development.
   Existentially we are ‘thrown’ into our lives at birth. We are also thrown out of our
lives in the sense that we do not know when we will die. On our path through life
from birth to death we start with something we are given, which is neither ours nor
requested (our individual existence) and our life task is to make it into something
which is personal and owned – only to lose it when we die. The most basic challenge
of life, of our development as human beings, is to find a way to live with this absurd-
ity and to learn to struggle, persevere and discover ways of being that get us what we
decide we need, bearing in mind that what we need changes as we go through life.
Existentially human development is a gradual, fluid but permanently cumulative and
meaning-based process from the sudden moment of birth to the uncertain moment
of death, and all people, no matter how young or old, or close to birth or to death,
continually strive for meaning and purpose and a lived understanding of the meaning
of the paradoxes and dilemmas of life. In this sense life is always incomplete, though
death forces a premature completeness on it because after death the person’s potential
ceases. Maturity, which is by no means guaranteed but is a measure of this active
understanding, is correlated more with life circumstances, conditions and choices than
with age (Adams, 2013b, 2014).
   Existentially therefore, children are no more and no less human beings than adults.
This is not to say that there is no difference between a child’s and an adult’s experi-
ence, but it is important to remember that the criteria we use as adults will have such
a strong cultural overlay as to make it hard to think clearly about precisely what these
differences may be. For example, in Europe the idea of a post-infancy childhood is
relatively recent and is an artefact of economics. Until the nineteenth century, after
the end of biological dependence, people were distinguished only by their physical
ability to work. While the 1870 Education Act in the UK brought in compulsory
education for children between 5 and 10, the age at which a (dependent) child
becomes an (independent) adult, which is formalized as the legal age of responsibility,
varies considerably in different cultures.
   One way childhood is objectified is in terms of the cultural dominance of the
idea that the child should go through fixed stages in order to reach maturity.
Looking at human development phenomenologically reveals something far more
complex and dynamic.
   We are born as pure existence and with no essence except for our bodily facticity
and our thrownness. Our consciousness is like a light we shine on the world in order
to create meaning and it is through this natural process of illumination that the child
begins to notice itself, others and its place in the world, and a sense of self begins to

                                       • 190 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


develop. This sense of self coalesces round the sense of an ‘original project’ and the
child’s almost infinite number of random, and largely incoherent, experiences come
to be reduced into a small number of coherent and prototypical ‘memories’ which are
actually highly selective and constructed. These epitomise our fundamental relation-
ship to others and to the world. This is the process whereby the infant gradually
acquires knowledge of itself in the world, and as Sartre (1997) says, infancy is a ‘crucial
event’. Because of its existential rather than cognitive nature, the original project is
more likely to be embodied and is about our place in the world – our stance to
dilemma, paradox and uncertainty.


Working with younger people

All human life is about flexibility and change but one obvious difference between
children and adults is that adults have simply had longer to ferment or sediment their
‘original project’ into the so-called facts of their personality in order to avoid the
anxiety of freedom and choice. Children have an immediacy of experience that is
forgotten and often pathologized and standardized by adults. Maybe as adults we have
become afraid of children’s flexibility, imagination and passion.
    In the movement from birth through to death a major task is to understand the
difference between those things one can or cannot take responsibility for, and this
involves understanding the world physically, socially, psychologically and ethically. A
child is involved in this as much, if not more than, an adult. Adults believe they have
understood it, children know they have not.
    The issue of responsibility is, if anything, even more important to acknowledge in
working with children and young people than it is with adults. Struggling with the
nature of autonomy and responsibility is an issue for children just as much as it is for
adults. Therapists must be even more aware of the ways they subtly guide and use sug-
gestion in their work with children so as not to leap in or jump in and take over the
child’s emergent autonomy.
    But there is another dimension to the issue of responsibility that impacts on our
work with children, and that is the legal dimension. A cornerstone of work with adults
is that they freely consent to the therapy contract. It is an ethical sine qua non that work
cannot start without it. The alternative is coercion or dishonesty. Adults are assumed to
be able to know what they are consenting to. Perhaps existentially, but certainly legally,
children do not have the ability to consent, so work with children will always involve
working with those, most often the parent(s), who will need to give their consent on
the child’s behalf. This means that unlike working with an adult who we can meet for
the first session without knowing anything more about them than their name, we will
always know a great deal about the child’s history and relationships from the point of
view of other people. We have to try to bracket this at the same time as understanding
the child as a relational product of their very particular context. Quite apart from the
child’s understanding of the concept of confidentiality almost certainly being different
from an adult’s understanding, the reality of who is entitled to know what about our
work with the child will also be different and will need to be negotiated. This makes
our work with children that much more complex as we are never just working with

                                         • 191 •
         • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


the child, although our allegiance should always be with the child in the context of
their life at present (Scalzo, 2010). This of course reflects the reality of the child’s life,
as the existential principle of being-in-the-world is a product of relationships and this
is never more true than for a child.This makes the tendency towards the objectification
and standardization of childhood even more important to resist.



                                       EXERCISE
                              Questions about childhood
   1.	   What does it mean for you to play? Describe what you do when you play?
   2.	   What is the difference between a child and an adult?
   3.	   Describe your childhood. When did it end?
   4.	   Would you like to work with children? What age? Why? Why not? What
         does this mean about how you see your own childhood?



We have already mentioned how central dialogue is to existential practice. The prin-
ciples that guide our understanding of adult behaviour are the same as those that guide
our understanding of child behaviour. These are that our sense of identity and under-
standing of life is drawn from our relationships, and the way we use language is inti-
mately bound up with these relationships.
   In our work with adults we use language without really thinking about it. We think
of dialogue and language as being almost synonymous. But they are not, and it is in
working with children that we realize this because our adult grasp of language is so
different – not necessarily greater – than theirs.This can be difficult in a verbal culture,
and adults can find it difficult to use language differently. It is truer to say that the use
of a common spoken language is not the only way that dialogue can develop, and the
first task of the counsellor working with children is to establish a language they can
both use and this will frequently not be a verbal, cognitive language, it will be an
embodied language, it will be a language of play, of drawing, of enactment. A shared
embodied language of play will aid communication and the development of dialogue.
As such it will not need to be interpreted; to do this is to misunderstand the nature
of language and will be likely to fracture the rapport. It will simply need to be under-
stood as a communication from one person to another. On many occasions by inter-
preting we will be attempting to put something into words that need not be put into
words, and importantly cannot be put into words. The dialogue that follows from
immersion and enactment in play will inevitably result in a freeing of the emerging
sedimentation, to allow the child to re-discover choice.


Working with older people

Therapeutic work with people at the other end of life makes an interesting contrast.
Not only do other therapeutic perspectives have even less to say about old age, but it

                                          • 192 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


does not have a status of a specialism either. It is so side-lined therapeutically that
practitioners are able to maintain an active and institutionalized bias against working
with the ‘old’ (Nelson, 2011). If anything, old people are more stereotyped, objectified
and standardized than young people. There has long been a widespread perception
(Kastenbaum, 1964) that working with older people is a poor investment of time and
that they would be unlikely to benefit from psychotherapy.
   In contrast to the ‘young’, the ‘old’ are hard to define.There is, for example, no legal
definition. Age brings no extra cultural privileges, only exemption from societal obli-
gation and this leads to a gradual disenfranchisement. There is even unease about
what word to use to refer to them. They are variously described as ‘old’, ‘elderly’,
‘elder’, ‘senior’, ‘pensioner’, ‘grandparent’, usually pejoratively or patronisingly. Old
people are complimented on how not-old, how young they look. Even researchers
cannot agree when old age begins (e.g. Hill and Brettle, 2006), but one UK marker
is having a bus pass, though this is not exclusive to old people and is also invisible.
Retirement is another marker, and although it is beginning to be researched
(e.g. Milne, 2013; Edwards and Milton, 2014) it is also invisible. In any case the retire-
ment age in the twenty-first century Western world is being pushed later for political
and economic reasons.
   Although the old are stereotyped as a homogenous group, in fact they are an
extraordinarily heterogeneous group and as policy makers realize, it is not an issue that
will go away and by 2035 more than one-third of the population of the UK will be
over 65. If, as some say, old age starts at 50, then the age span of old age can be as
much as 40 or more years – nearly two generations. In 2015, the average age expecta-
tion in the UK for women is 82 and 78 for men and rising. There are also enormous
individual and socio-economic differences between individuals, with some older
people being well and high-functioning while others 20 or 30 years younger may be
showing signs of frailty (Munk, 2010). Because of social conditioning, men who are
more likely to have worked consistently throughout their lives will have greater prob-
lems using the freedom of retirement. Women, on the other hand, who have been
carers will find there is always someone else to care for: children, then parents, then
grandchildren, then partners. Also personal appearance will be less of an issue for men
than for women. Simone de Beauvoir’s book The Coming of Age (1970) has described
all these issues very well and forms a good basis from which to understand ageing
existentially and working with older people in therapy.
   It is important when working with people at the end of life to help them formulate
their own perceptions and experiences of how many gains and losses they are suffer-
ing. While some may consider the wisdom they have accumulated to outweigh their
losses and aches and pains, others may be very preoccupied with the process of strug-
gle with decay and decline. Existential ideas challenge the loss-deficit model, but we
must also acknowledge that the opposite counter-myth that over-emphasizes the
positive elements of getting old may be fuelled by the therapist’s own death denial.
This is a young person’s prejudice. Working with people at the far end of life requires
the same balanced and open approach that all existential therapy requires. It will focus
on finding out how each different individual is able to understand the many experi-
ences they have had in their lives and how they are preparing themselves to harvest
that learning in preparation for the final summary of the ending.

                                        • 193 •
         • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •



                                     EXERCISE
                               Questions about ageing
   1.	   Imagine yourself in 30 years time. What are you like?
   2.	   What assumptions do you have of ageing?
   3.	   What fears do you have of your ageing?
   4.	   When did you first realize you were getting old?



We said earlier that just as an emphasis on the beginning of the work is echoed in an
emphasis on the beginning of life in therapeutic theory, the downplaying of the end
of the work is echoed by the end of life being ignored. Laura Barnett’s book When
Death Enters the Therapeutic Space (2008) is one of the few that takes this issue on.
Moreover, and in spite of the IAPT intention, people aged 65 and over are not only
an under-represented client group (0.8 per cent of all referrals in 2014–15) but they
had the highest recovery rate (Health and Social Care Information Centre, 2015).
Most work is with people who are of the same generation or younger than the coun-
sellor. It is hard not to put this down to a wider cultural avoidance of death and end-
ings. Research has corroborated this on a personal level and has shown (Woolfe and
Biggs, 1997) how therapists’ reluctance to work with old people reflects their personal
fears about their own ageing and mortality. Nevertheless, having exposure to and
knowledge about working with older adults will give them the opportunity to con-
front their own anxieties and fears about ageing and mortality.
   Rather than thinking that the issues older people bring to therapy are connected
directly to death and dying, it is better to reframe them as issues associated with the
everyday challenges of a life being lived in a context in which less time remains than
has been lived.
   The existential perspective is able to address the needs of individuals approaching
boundary situations like loss, death, isolation, as well as developmental milestones like
retirement because it can offer an alternative framework to the traditional models that
emphasize only pathology, achievement and adjustment.
   In one sense though, the work is no different than with any other client group.
Phenomenologically we work with every individual as they are, regardless of their
chronological age. Having said that, because phenomenology is sensitive to context
and to individuals it is inevitable that there will be some differences. For a start, some
people in their seventies and eighties might just not know what therapy is or assume
it to be about psychoanalysis. This will mean that there will be a need for a less for-
mal and consequently a more active engagement to gain an understanding of what
the client wants to achieve through therapy. What may start as an ordinary counsel-
ling relationship may become more like a pastoral friendship which both enjoy and
benefit from.
   Existential change is always related to humility in the face of the givens, and the
skill and confidence of the counsellor is correlated with their capacity for humility
in the face of death. Contradicting directly the prejudice that old people are


                                        • 194 •
 • • •  Applications of Existential Counselling and Psychotherapy  • • •


unlikely to benefit from psychotherapy, Orbach (1999) and Yalom (2011, 2015) say
that probably at no other time in life is there as potent a force towards self-awareness
operating as in old age. Confrontation with death and dying will invariably evoke a
reassessment of life’s achievements and values for both therapist and client on a par
with the most effective therapy. In this sense old people have an uncanny way of
exposing a central existential dilemma of life. Therapists will get to understand that
talking about death can never be theoretical, it will always be about ‘my death’
(Cooper and Adams, 2005).
    As de Beauvoir (1970) said, ‘We must stop cheating. The whole meaning of our life
is in question in the future that is waiting for us. […] let us recognize ourselves in this
old man or in that old woman.’
    It is also likely that this process of self-questioning will begin not just in connection
with our own sense of mortality but in relation to loss in general. The greatest exis-
tential problem of all is the encounter with approaching death. The way individuals
deal with this loss is directly related to their earlier experiences of loss, and this prin-
ciple applies equally to client and therapist. If the therapist is reluctant to ask herself
questions like ‘How would I live if I had only three months left?’ or ‘When am I going
to retire?’, their avoidance will only compound the client’s sense of inner isolation.
The more anxious the therapist is about ageing, the more likely they will stereotype
the older client to avoid having to acknowledge their own future death. And the more
vulnerable older people are, the greater the anxiety younger people will have about
death, which will in turn increase their tendency to pathologize ageing into socially
acceptable ageism.
    A major issue will be the assumptions the therapist and client have of each other
with respect to their relative ages. The therapist may be uneasy talking to someone the
same age as their parents, and the client may be uneasy talking to someone the same
age as their children. For the therapist this will inevitably bring up thoughts about
their own and their parents’ ageing and death, and for the client it will bring up
thoughts of when they were a generation younger with more years ahead. It is impor-
tant for the therapist to be ready to learn from their clients about their experience of
a different generation. This is one of the biggest obstacles in working with older peo-
ple: the fact that the work will impact deeply on the therapist. Paradoxically the ‘old’
are more able to address ageing than the young, and if they are open to it the young
have a great deal to learn about ageing from the old. Not only should the therapist
aiming to work with older adults be aware of their own beliefs and biases towards
older people, but they must also take the time to gain an understanding of the exis-
tential reality of old age.
    Längle notes that:

  Man ages the way he has lived. […] everything that was only appears in more
  clear cut shapes in old age. To be able to see and accept this requires that we find
  our own personal answers to the basic questions of being human […] Only then
  can we talk to them without falling silent, and only then can we accompany
  them, stand by them and guide them, make them develop their last personal
  touch. (2001: 195)


                                         • 195 •
       • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •



 KEY POINTS

 •• Therapists must be aware of the ways they use suggestion in their work with
    children so as not to take over the child’s emergent autonomy.
 •• Both young people and old people are as likely to be stereotyped, objecti-
    fied, and standardized.
 •• Developing dialogue through a common language involves more than just
    words.
 •• Neither a loss-deficit model of ageing nor its denial is a useful base for
    effective therapy.
 •• Confrontation with death and dying will invariably evoke a reassessment of
    life’s achievements and values for both therapist and client.
 •• The therapist aiming to work with older adults must be aware of their own
    beliefs and biases towards older people.
