                PROFESSIONAL
                 ISSUES AND
                 CHALLENGES


  ‘Difficulties strengthen the mind, as labour does the body.’
                                                                                  Seneca



THE CHALLENGE OF WORKING IN THE NHS

Existential therapy has an ambiguous relationship to working in public health services.
As existential therapists approach mental health in a free and open philosophical man-
ner they may find it difficult to fit into a system that is based on a medical model. As
mental health services evolve, it may become possible to include an existential way of
working. The disparity between the treatment of physical and mental health has long
been an issue in statutory health and social care provision and the last decade has seen
a rapid expansion in the therapeutic services offered or sponsored by government
agencies.
   The Improving Access to Psychological Therapies (IAPT) programme and the associ-
ated ‘Talking Therapies’ four-year plan that started in 2011 in the UK aimed to improve
access to psychological therapies for adults presenting with depression or anxiety, and it
also aimed to increase uptake by people of over 65 years as this age group is under-rep-
resented. Moreover these programmes were to be combined where appropriate with
medication, which previously was the only option. In 2014–2015 of the 1.2 million
individuals who were referred to IAPT services, 0.8 million entered treatment. Of these,
0.4 million completed treatment at average length of six sessions. (Health & Social Care
Information Centre, 2015). The programme acknowledges the link between mental and
physical health by aiming to include people with long-term physical health conditions,
medically unexplained symptoms and severe mental illness. It also places emphasis not just
on the use of psychological treatments that have an established evidence base, but also on
the individual therapeutic needs of clients and their social context.The research evidence
suggests that when given the choice most people would opt for counselling rather than
antidepressant medication (Schaik et  al., 2004) In addition, The Royal College of
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


Psychiatrists and the Royal College of General Practitioners (2008) have argued that ‘an
active preference by individuals for the method used is associated with better out-
comes’. This has been echoed by Professor David Clark (Philosophy for Life, 2013).
   This emphasis on therapeutic practices that have produced research to back up
effectiveness has led the National Institute for Clinical Excellence (NICE) to give a
formal backing to CBT-based therapeutic practices, while acknowledging the validity
of any modalities that can provide evidence of effectiveness.
   Working on the idea that therapy is not ‘one size fits all’, an important feature of the
IAPT programme is accurate assessment and diagnosis and with this in mind, delivery
of IAPT programmes is divided into low-intensity and high-intensity interventions.
   Low-intensity interventions are designed for mild to moderate anxiety and depres-
sion and involve guided self-help with materials including bibliotherapy, homework
and problem-solving techniques, and because they are more didactic can be thought
of as psycho-education rather than psychotherapy. Appointment times are likely to be
less than 50 minutes or weekly, and in addition to being face to face may be delivered
via the telephone, synchronous chat or asynchronous email, Skype contact or by the
clients’ use of pre-existing computer programs.These programs are delivered by mem-
bers of a new group of professionals called psychological wellbeing practitioners
(PWPs) trained specifically for the purpose.
   High-intensity interventions are designed for moderate to severe anxiety and
depression and people may be referred to a high-intensity programme after having
been on a low-intensity programme and having shown greater need than was first
thought. A high-intensity programme will still be short term but will typically be face
to face. The client will also have an option to choose the treatment they feel is best
for them. Trained psychological therapists deliver high-intensity interventions.
   Another important characteristic of IAPT work is that consistent with the principle
of evidence-based practice, the entire programme is assessed and evaluated by each
session being monitored with the use of a questionnaire known as the CORE form.
   Many of these characteristics (very short-term contracts, diagnosis, questionnaire-
based monitoring, quantitative evaluation and prescribed aims) would on the face of
it present challenges that an existential therapist would find it hard to work with.
   Notwithstanding the issues around gathering evidence of effectiveness, which are
looked at in the next section on research, these issues are actually not so difficult for
existential therapists to engage with as might at first be thought.
   As has been said, existential therapy is based on the practice of phenomenology,
which itself is a research method. Unlike some methods, the fact that phenomenology
is not rigidly formalized is a strength rather than a weakness in that as long as the
principles are adhered to, phenomenology is flexible enough to adapt itself to any
setting whose values do not contradict the principles of phenomenology as referred
to in Chapter 3.
   With respect to contract length, existentially informed and trained therapists and
counselling psychologists will be practised at modifying their technique to fit the
client, the context and the conditions. Existential therapists have been working in
and managing such settings effectively for many years and have been adapting exis-
tential phenomenological practice accordingly and finding ways to work without
sacrificing their values.

                                        • 198 •
                • • •  Professional Issues and Challenges  • • •


   With respect to diagnosis and questionnaire-based monitoring and evaluation, the
point was made earlier that while existential therapists will question the medical prin-
ciples of diagnosis, the practical aim is always to investigate the meaning of the diag-
nosis for the client.Therapeutic technique will therefore be modified according to the
dynamics of the particular relationship, not to the diagnosis. The point was made
earlier that not only is assessment and monitoring of some sort always being done, but
assessment and monitoring that focuses on meaning and understanding is an integral
part of existential practice, and has also been found to promote client motivation and
hence improvement. It is also a professional necessity.
   With respect to the prescribed aims of high-intensity work, although one of the
rationales for the IAPT programme is socio-economic (i.e. to reduce the demand on
the benefits system and help a return to work), one of the consequences of this is the
existential value referred to earlier, of encouraging economic self-sufficiency. It is
important to note though that the socio-economic aim of a return to economic self-
sufficiency is an aim of the programme as a whole rather than an aim imposed upon
each patient in each session. Nor are the aims of IAPT solely behavioural, or doing
based; the items on the CORE forms are the meaning or being-based questions that
all existential therapists base their work around.
   Existentially, living is about doing as well as being, and the aim of existential therapy
is that the client is able to get on with living their life more effectively and with
greater meaning and purpose. This is promoted through open and direct dialogue in
a way that illuminates for the client the way they live out their values, attitudes, beliefs
and assumptions. This echoes NICE guidelines, which acknowledge that although
CBT-based therapies are preferenced, they do not replace therapists’ knowledge and
skills (DoH, 2010).
   Moreover, third-wave CBT practices like dialectical behaviour therapy (DBT) and
the use of mindfulness for the prevention of recurrence of depression pay far more
attention to relational factors in CBT than formerly, and this is shown by phenome-
nology being included in the curriculum of high intensity therapists (DoH, 2011).



   KEY POINTS

   •• Phenomenology is flexible enough to adapt itself to any setting whose val-
      ues do not contradict the principles of phenomenology.
   •• While existential therapists question the medical principles of diagnosis,
      the practical aim is always to investigate the meaning of the diagnosis for
      the client.


RESEARCH AND EXISTENTIAL THERAPY

Fundamentally, researching is about asking questions and seeking answers. It is about
testing the usefulness of theory and finding out what the facts about something are. A
commitment to research is a characteristic of reflective practice, and is also a part of

                                         • 199 •
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


existential philosophy’s practical search for truth and love of wisdom. Existential
therapy is founded on the principles of phenomenological research, which provide a
methodology to investigate the nature and process of therapy. Phenomenology is
equally important to psychological research.
    Existential therapy is an enquiry into meaning, and any enquiry that is not rigorous
and systematic will lead to results that are influenced by what the researcher wishes to
find. Phenomenologically this is about the influence of unexamined assumptions. In
research terms it is called the ‘allegiance effect’, where results frequently bear a similar-
ity to the therapeutic orientation of the researchers. We need to have a way to ensure
that our theory, our way of conceptualizing what we do, is derived from practice, not
from our assumptions about practice.
    Finlay (2011) notes that in spite of existential counselling being based on phenom-
enology there has been surprisingly little research into its effectiveness and qualities,
and this is partly because of the technical difficulties in designing the research.
However, formal existential research into outcome is beginning to change (Vos et al.,
2015; Rayner and Vitali, 2014), with the emergence of a generation of existential
counselling psychologists working in the NHS who have been trained in both quali-
tative and quantitative research methods.
    Research in the general sense of monitoring, evaluating and reflecting on experi-
ence is what we do all the time as reflective human beings in order to make the
continuity of the past, the present and the future more coherent. As such our practice
is influenced by our experiences outside therapy, our experience as a client, and our
supervision. This evidence is a result of our personal experience and is a valuable
source of knowledge, but there are other sources which are from outside our experi-
ence: the theory that underpins our training; and from the findings of formal research.
    In our training we are encouraged to read, understand and discuss theory but we
also need to be able to read, understand and discuss research findings. Just as our prac-
tice should be theory informed rather than theory directed, so should it be research
informed rather than research directed. This is consistent with the American
Psychological Association’s definition of evidence-based practice as ‘the integration of
the best available research with clinical expertise in the context of patient character-
istics, culture and preferences’ (APA, 2006: 273). On a personal level, by researching
into our own practice we monitor our effectiveness as therapists. We should always be
reading both theory and research and pushing the boundaries of our personal knowl-
edge. Consistent with the wider vision of the existential modality, therapists should
also be reading outside the narrow confines of theory and philosophy and reading
fiction as well. Research has corroborated the widely held intuition that fiction broad-
ens the mind (Kidd and Castano, 2013). We have said before that what we find is a
function of what we expect to find and this applies to research in terms of what is
researched into as well as what is found. But we have to start from somewhere and
rather than looking to confirm previous findings, attitudes, viewpoints, or prejudices
we need to look for phenomena that challenge or contradict our assumptions.
    One way we can do this is by keeping records of our clinical work and by using
these in supervision so as to stay critically aware of what we do and what we do not
yet know about it.This leads us to focus on the questions that are still remaining rather
those that have already been answered. Also communicating with others about what

                                         • 200 •
                • • •  Professional Issues and Challenges  • • •


we do prompts us to open our practice up to question, and this in turn prompts us to
examine our own practice from new directions.
   Existential counsellors have traditionally been sceptical of quantitative research
because of its natural science foundation and its focus on reduction and causation.The
argument against such research was that every course of counselling or therapy is
unique. While this is undoubtedly true, it is also true that we all have our humanity
in common. The trend towards evidence-based practice makes the debate about what
makes therapy effective particularly relevant, and Wampold (2001) suggests a combina-
tion of technical and relational factors that are common across all counselling models.
This has also been described as the ‘Dodo bird verdict’ in which ‘all have won and
everyone must have prizes’, and while this argument has some appeal it has led to the
reductive and simplistic conclusion that all practices are as effective as each other and
takes us little further on than we were at the start. Lambert and Barley (2001) go
further and say that 40 per cent of therapeutic successes are due to client attributable
factors, with the therapy relationship representing 30 per cent and therapeutic tech-
nique representing only 15 per cent. Clearly more rigorous research needs to be done.
   Cooper (2008, 2015) argues strongly for more research on the basis that there is
no doubt that evidence-based practice is better than theory or imagination-based
practice. But existentially to think that there will ever be an answer to the specific/
non-specific factors question is a technological way of thinking. The questions of
existential therapy are the same as the questions of life, and while we can find out
many fascinating and relevant things, essentially life is a mystery that has no end and
we need to acknowledge that there are limits to our knowledge. Research, both
formal and informal, can only ever give a clue to the likelihood, the probability, that
such-and-such an event will happen. It can never bring certainty. Notwithstanding
the existential ideological objections to quantitative research, Cooper (2015) says
that a stance of ‘methodological pluralism’ that accommodates the best aspects of
both qualitative and quantitative research would better reflect the existential posi-
tion of openness along with a need to engage with the world of health care in the
twenty-first century.
   With respect to formal research, there is a wide disparity between the amount of
medical research and psychological research. There is little commercial appeal in
researching psychological therapies and statutory academic and health care institutions
are unlikely to have the budgets required to fund research, which tends to be enor-
mously complex and to have huge ethical implications. Nevertheless, although psy-
chological interventions are qualitatively different from medical interventions, it has
led to the same criteria being used to assess psychological research as are used to assess
medical research. This may work for short-term interventions when they are used
instead of drug treatment, but does not work for medium- or long-term therapy.
NICE guidance has historically relied upon randomized controlled trials (RCTs) as
the studies that produce the evidence based upon which guidelines are created for
treatment. According to these widely available criteria (Evans, 2003), the research
judged as ‘excellent’ is deemed to be the systematic review of large numbers of similar
pieces of research. The next best is the RCT. Interpretive research is judged ‘good’ and
descriptive research as ‘fair’. Significantly for psychotherapy training, the case study
research method is judged as ‘poor’.

                                        • 201 •
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


   RCTs are extremely costly and complex to run, but the huge amount of mate-
rial generated by CORE forms will make it possible to do research of this sort.
But even so, such research will only pertain to symptomatic change and not to
existential changes. In the meantime the more readily measurable outcomes of
such treatments as CBT have proven to make such therapies to be the most
attractive candidates for quantitative research. This is the main reason why they
have been favoured for the IAPT programme alongside interpersonal psycho-
therapy (IPT) and dynamic interpersonal therapy (DIT). Quantitative research
into the effectiveness of existential therapy has been slow in coming but some
studies have been done that show encouraging results. Rayner and Vitali (2014)
for example, using the CORE goal attainment form, showed that a significant
number (80 per cent) of their sample attained their stated first goals between
‘moderately well’ or above.
   This research is an example of how outcome research can be done on existential
counselling and produce transferable results that cannot just meet the rigorous stand-
ards of research but also may meet NICE guidelines for research.



COUNSELLING PSYCHOLOGY – THE
RELATIONSHIP OF COUNSELLING TO THE
PSYCHOLOGY PROFESSION

Psychology and psychotherapy have had until recently a largely separate but parallel
history. Psychology started as a discrete discipline in the mid-nineteenth century and
has been closely allied to the natural sciences. Psychology has always had a strong
research base and as such the theory that was developed was always derived from
quantitative research. Because of this psychology has been primarily concerned with
the measurement and recording of behaviour and in the early days this led to the
development of learning theory, and then to the development of behaviourism as a
discrete discipline. Most of the earlier experiments were done on mice, rats or pigeons,
the principle being that all animals – and humans are animals too – learn behaviour
in the same way. If these principles could be discovered, then they could be applied
to human beings. While there were some early applications of learning theory (e.g. in
psychiatric hospitals where token economies were introduced), psychology has tended
to be a rather academic discipline.
   The emergence of the humanistic tradition in the 1940s was a reaction to the ten-
dency of both psychoanalysis and behaviourism to see the person as essentially passive
and reactive. The humanistic tradition wanted to return to the person and aimed to
make psychology a human science rather than a natural science. Meanwhile and inde-
pendently of both psychology and counselling and psychotherapy, existential philoso-
phy had been advocating an experience-based study of the person since the
mid-nineteenth century in the work of Kierkegaard and Nietzsche.
   In the mid-1970s there was a radical change of emphasis in psychological research
and a far greater importance was acknowledged for the role of thinking, of cogni-
tions, in the understanding of behaviour. This paralleled not just the movement of

                                       • 202 •
                • • •  Professional Issues and Challenges  • • •


psychology into the therapeutic field, but also the rise of the cognitive therapies in
the world of counselling and psychotherapy.
   This laid the foundations in the UK for the more recent development of counsel-
ling psychology. The Division of Counselling Psychology within the British
Psychological Society held its first annual conference in 1990, and one of us (EvD)
gave the founding talk on philosophical underpinnings of counselling psychology,
in which she emphasized the importance of phenomenological methods. The
Division is concerned with the integration of psychological theory and research
with therapeutic practice and has continuously supported the development of phe-
nomenological research. In 2014 there were 13 institutions in the UK offering a
doctorate-level counselling psychology training, including the New School of
Psychotherapy and Counselling (NSPC) and Regent’s University, both in London
and both founded on phenomenological principles. Counselling psychology does
not favour any particular therapeutic perspective. Instead it takes a more practical
and pragmatic view and favours empirical models of practice and research with a
base in the primacy of the therapeutic relationship. Many of the 13 trainings include
a substantial existential component alongside other perspectives. Counselling psy-
chology stays close to the research tradition of psychology but instead of being based
just in quantitative natural science research it also accommodates the qualitative
phenomenological research tradition.
   Both Orlans and van Scoyoc (2008) and Cooper (2009) emphasize the practical
values of counselling psychology, and Kasket (2011) reinforces this by referring to
counselling psychology as a ‘particularly honest, realistic, courageous member of the
family of Applied Psychologies’. This point about application is significant and echoes
a point made earlier in this book about existential counselling being the practical
application of philosophy to everyday living. It is also a point made by Sartre in
Existentialism and Humanism (1973) which positioned existentialism as a practical dis-
cipline to address the process and contextual nature of human issues.
   Counselling psychology also embodies a philosophically orientated set of values
(Goldstein, 2009), and its competencies are founded on six principles:

1.	 A prioritization of the client’s subjective, and intersubjective, experience.
2.	 A focus on facilitating growth and the actualization of potential rather than treat-
    ing pathology.
3.	 An orientation towards empowering clients rather than seeing empowerment as
    an adjunct to an absence of mental illness.
4.	 A commitment to a democratic, non-hierarchical client–therapist relationship.
5.	 An appreciation of the client as a unique being rather than as an instance of uni-
    versal laws.
6.	 An understanding of the client as a socially and relationally embedded being.
    (Orlans and van Scoyoc, 2008)

These principles align counselling psychology strongly with the existential tradition
in that the overall aim of the existential counselling psychologist is to work with the

                                        • 203 •
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


client in their search for their own truth throughout their life with an open mind and
an attitude of wonder, rather than fitting the client overtly or covertly into established
frameworks of interpretation (Milton, 2010, 2012, 2014).
   By preferencing human meaning, both counselling psychology and existentialism
acknowledge a plurality of viewpoints and a variety of potential meanings.
   What counselling psychology can bring to psychotherapy and counselling is a
broader understanding of human nature and also an understanding of the value of
research in developing theory and practice.
   What counselling psychology and existential therapy have in common in terms of
therapeutic practice and skills are their shared ethical values and their commitment to
phenomenological practice.



THE CHALLENGE OF WORKING WITH
INTERNET-BASED TECHNOLOGY

The emergence of internet-based technology has not only transformed our lives but
also the way we think about and practise psychotherapy. It is reasonable to suppose
that the more familiar people become with this technology, the more it will become
integrated into everyday life and also therapeutic practice.
   In the world of psychotherapy and counselling, technological approaches have
developed relatively slowly and are sometimes viewed with suspicion. The debate has
often been cast in the mould of face to face (f2f) versus online, with the idea that there
is no substitute for two people being in the same room at the same time. This way of
thinking supposed that to be as good, online therapy should be able to reproduce all
elements of f2f work. This has been shown to be not only not so, but it has also
prompted more research into what is therapeutic about the therapeutic relationship.
   From this perspective f2f and online counselling are not viewed as separate and
distinct, but as variations of the same phenomenon – a relationship – and that online
counselling is considered as a new, versatile and flexible resource with the potential to
complement and support f2f work and vice versa. Each has its own advantages and
drawbacks.
   Working with internet-based technology has been embraced by existential practi-
tioners who have used the possibilities offered in their practice as well as in delivering
training courses, for example at the New School of Psychotherapy and Counselling
(NSPC).
   There is not space here for a review of the impact of these technologies on the way
we live so we will confine ourselves here to the constraints and opportunities offered
by them on our counselling practice.
   Technology has always mediated human communication from the use of gongs to the
printing press and beyond.Telephone counselling has for many years been a part of coun-
selling practice, but what is new is the range of other mediums. Included in these are the
use of webcams, synchronous and asynchronous chat sessions and texting. As has been
noted, the most obvious contrast with conventional practice is geographical location, but
this does not present as large an obstacle as it may at first seem. While Heidegger talked

                                        • 204 •
                • • •  Professional Issues and Challenges  • • •


about the capacity for television to abolish remoteness and while he foresaw that it
would ‘soon pervade and dominate the whole machinery of communication’ (1977:
163), he would probably have been shocked and surprised by the ability of the Internet
to diminish time and space in a far more dramatic way than television. Internet-based
media in fact have the capacity to connect us in a way that better reflects our being-
in-the-world with others. ‘Being together’ does not necessitate physical proximity
because through the Internet with its capacity to shrink space we can ‘be together’ with
others wherever they are. In this sense ‘being-in-the-web’ is just another way of being-
in-the-world. While technology may enhance and modify modes of communication,
the fundamental human need for relatedness and mutual understanding endures and
finds ways to manifest itself.
    Heidegger also reminds us that we do not simply live ‘in space’. The way he puts it
is that we are spatial; it is a part of our existential nature to be both in and of space. A
consequence of this is that in the absence of the physically shared space, a different
kind of space develops, a shared virtual space, which is no less or more imaginary that
the shared psychic space than happens in conventional face-to-face work. In a sense
this resembles the space that develops in the psychoanalytic use of the couch but with
greater equality between the therapist and client. This virtual space has been called
‘telepresence’ by Rochlen et al. and is the ‘feeling of being in someone else’s presence
without sharing their physical space’ (2004: 272).
    Regardless of medium and whether the therapist uses internet technology in their
counselling work, this theme of increased equality and transparency is reinforced by
both therapists and clients alike having a web presence independently of their rela-
tionship as therapist and client. It is almost inevitable that a client will look up their
therapist on the Internet to find out more about them, and probably vice versa.
    Every change to the therapeutic frame, whether in terms of space, time or tech-
nique, has the effect of challenging assumptions about therapy but also presents dif-
ferent advantages and disadvantages which impose modifications to practice. Being a
research method and essentially flexible, there is no reason why phenomenology
cannot be modified to take all these changes into account.
    The two main categories of internet-based counselling are synchronous sound and
vision with the use of webcams, and text-based chatrooms, which can be synchronous
or asynchronous on a forum. There are in addition many other creative new aids that
can assist with online therapy, which are widely available; for instance, psychology-
based games, tests, chatbots, self-help materials, a 24/7 connection to international
peer support, phone mood trackers, text-based methods to pick up on mood or sui-
cidality, and of course methods of tracking the progress of therapy.
    So there are many reasons why people feel the benefit of these new therapeutic
resources.
    The most common reason though is convenience (Chester and Glass, 2006), as
online therapy can overcome barriers like limited mobility due to geographical isola-
tion, physical disability or time availability. Additionally some people who feel great
shame about their troubles may find it easier to use impersonal methods to talk to
someone. The point about geography is particularly significant existentially and
becomes more significant the greater the geographical distance. Counsellors who
practise phenomenologically will be familiar in their professional and their personal

                                         • 205 •
         • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


lives with the importance of bracketing and understanding the meaning of their
assumptions and prejudices. Freed up from working in a single geographical location
will mean that counsellor and client will more than likely be from very different cul-
tures, as well as working in very different cultures. Not working in the same geo-
graphical location will also mean that the people will have a variable facility with a
common language. This will bring home the existential fact of the relativity of values
and will force the counsellor or supervisor to question their assumptions about reality
and existential issues. Other stated reasons are privacy, the advantages of relative ano-
nymity, and ambivalence about f2f counselling based on the traditional social stigmas
about seeking help.
   Suler (2004) has noted that people tend to behave with less restraint in cyberspace
and those who actively choose non-f2f mediums find that the lack of social cues pro-
motes rather than inhibits greater feelings of safety (Leibert and Archer, 2006). A fac-
tor in promoting this disinhibition will be the fact that more than likely the client will
be talking to the therapist from the comfort of their own home, rather than the
therapist’s home or a neutral space of the therapist’s choosing. What happens is that
the loss of visual cues leads to others becoming more acute. It is as if the telepresence
allows clients to experiment, to disclose things that usually remain hidden and to
reflect on their responses in their own time and at their own pace. This is further
enhanced in text-based work where there is a permanent record of the session. It is
possible then that individuals who are socially isolated and anxious are more able to
access counselling online than f2f. However, familiarity with counselling as accessed
online may prompt a shift in preconceptions about f2f encounters, leading some cli-
ents to seek out f2f opportunities to test out their new-found ability to relate to
people in a way they have previously avoided. There is considerable evidence that
online counselling is effective for young people if only because of their familiarity
with the technology.
   It is of course possible that anonymity could lead to the client engaging in identity
and impression management, but this is not necessarily unique to online counselling.
   The use of webcams makes counselling work more similar to f2f, but it still remains
more convenient and geographically easier. Although there are fewer social cues avail-
able on webcams, they are still more than on text-based mediums so anonymity and
disinhibition, while present, will not be such a factor. Webcam-based work is also
dependent on the quality of the technology. Grainy, pixilated pictures, feedback on the
sound and intermittent breaking up of the signal can make it hard to work effectively.
There are also confidentiality issues if public servers are being used.



                                     EXERCISE
   1.	   What is your personal history of writing?
   2.	   When do you use writing as a way to understand yourself?
   3.	   What is your experience of internet-based therapy or supervision?
   4.	   What part does internet-based technology play in your life?




                                        • 206 •
                • • •  Professional Issues and Challenges  • • •


As has been stated, although it may seem counterintuitive, text-based work has many
advantages because of and not in spite of its difference from f2f work.
   The use of writing, including journal writing (Thompson, 2010), in therapy too has
a long and established history, and there is a wide body of evidence supporting the
effectiveness of addressing emotional difficulties through writing.
   Text-based counselling work is usually thought about in terms of whether the work
is synchronous (i.e. the counsellor and client agree to meet for a chat session of a
particular length at a particular time) or asynchronous (i.e. when the time delay
between the counsellor posting text and the client responding may be variable). This
pacing is a bit more like email communication. In practice there are more similarities
than differences.
   The sense of mind-to-mind meeting, free of visual cues and scrutiny encourages
disinhibition and the generation of a sense of telepresence which allows participants
to reflect on responses at their own pace, and because the session is written it offers
the unique opportunity to return and re-experience the actual dialogue as and when
participants choose. This allows both counsellor and client more opportunity to
understand the process and content than f2f work would. When it is working well,
both the counsellor and client will develop a unique personal shorthand written
language.
   As the relational autobiographical history is written down, the use of text makes
the work similar to narrative approaches because it facilitates the close examination of
the development of a personal narrative. It allows for an examination similar to that
of the research method of conversational discourse analysis (CDA).
   With respect to equality and transparency, because the client is in greater control of
the content and depth of their material and also the pacing of the session, this can
foster a greater sense of psychological safety. For counsellors unused to this dynamic
it can be unnerving. Usually typing appears on the screen only when the send button
is clicked and because no new typing appears on the screen can lead to both people
not knowing if the other person is there and thinking, there and waiting or there and
blank. This is uncertainty, and the ambiguity in the no-reply can become a blank
screen where one can easily project one’s own expectations, emotions and anxieties.
One has to learn to trust the void. For both client and counsellor it can be either a
fertile ground for anxiety and feelings of abandonment or alternatively for the free-
dom of shared intimacy. It is extremely challenging and demands 100 per cent atten-
tion from both the counsellor and client in order that the intersubjective experience
of telepresence not be broken.
   Another point about text-based work is a consequence of the obvious fact that it
takes longer to type than to speak. This means that both counsellor and client need to
learn to be concise and to the point.This too can be challenging for some counsellors,
but learning to distil thoughts accurately into writing is a skill well worth refining and
is transferable to f2f work. Some online therapy is conducted by email exchange,
where certain dates are set for replies, allowing for more measured interventions. This
can also be a useful method of supporting very distressed clients who come for one
f2f session with an additional allocation of an hour of the therapist’s email time per
week. Such arrangements can work well, but require clear boundaries.


                                        • 207 •
       • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


Other online presence in existential
therapy

The NSPC has been a partner in a series of European grant-based projects to establish
online training of existential therapists. These projects have been supported by the
European Union and have generated a considerable amount of research on self-disclosure
and disinhibition in this format. Online existential materials can trigger in-depth discus-
sion and dialogue, both in asynchronous and synchronous format. The ability to refer
directly to online philosophical and psychological resources adds a dimension of depth to
the explorations (see Deurzen et al., 2006, 2008).



   KEY POINTS

   •• F2f and online approaches should not be viewed as separate and distinct, but
      as different ways of manifesting our being-in-the-world.
   •• Both f2f and online counselling have advantages and disadvantages.
   •• Telepresence is the feeling of being in someone else’s presence without
      sharing their physical space.
   •• One of the advantages of internet-based work is that fewer social cues allow
      disinhibition and anonymity.
   •• Because of the lack of social cues, internet-based work can be challenging
      for the therapist.

                                          10

                   SUMMING UP:
              Putting it all
                 together


  ‘What I really need is to get clear about what I must do, not what I must know; the
  crucial thing is to find a truth which is truth for me, to find the idea for which I
  am willing to live and die.’
                                                                    Søren Kierkegaard



SUMMARIZING THE PHILOSOPHICAL BASIS
OF EXISTENTIAL PRACTICE

Although people wishing to work existentially do not necessarily need to know the
philosophy in great detail, they do need some discipline and method in how to think
philosophically.
   There has long been a debate about how to define existential philosophy, and it is in
the nature of existential thinking that it will always remain controversial. It will always
give us food for thought. The problem of definition is an important one, but one we
should be careful with. It is important in that we need to know who we are and what
our principles are, otherwise it can get too amorphous and end up meaning everything
and nothing at the same time. But it is also a mistake to think we can nail it down once
and for all. We cannot. Life is not like that. A classification is like a map, selective, pro-
visional and an abstraction, and attempts to distinguish by rigid lines need to be taken
with a degree of scepticism. All the interesting and valuable things happen when one
thing meets another, not when things are kept separate. The defining principles such as
they are can only be philosophical and epistemological and not simply technical or
behavioural. If we try to define existential therapy in terms of technique, little useful
will come of it. Classifying animals by appearance and habitat will put bats and birds,
moles and earthworms, fish and whales in the same groups when bats, moles and whales
have more in common with each other than, respectively, birds, earthworms and fish.
We need to be sure what we are talking about so that we can compare like with like.
The principles we have adopted for the purpose of this book are listed below.
       • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


   Existential philosophy and phenomenology are nothing if not context sensitive,
and what this means is that the ways these principles are operationalized (i.e. lived)
will be down to the individual and the way they construct their meaning and pur-
pose in their particular time and place and in the light of their own conscience and
understanding.
   Because existential ideas and phenomenology are context sensitive they will by
definition have different qualities at different times and places. Like the self, these ideas
gain resilience and truth by existing and adapting to different circumstances.
   These epistemological principles are:

1.	 Existence comes before essence. Existential therapy has a primary concern with
    human existence rather than with various essences or so-called personality char-
    acteristics. That we are determines who we are, not the other way round.
2.	 This being the case, a person’s identity, how they think about themselves and how
    they write their own autobiography becomes a matter of personal interpretation,
    responsibility and choice.
3.	 Every body of knowledge – and existential therapy is a body of knowledge – has
    to have a research method consistent with its principles and phenomenology, with
    its belief in the value of experience-near reflexive description, is the appropriate
    method to investigate existential therapy and other human sciences.


SUMMARIZING THE PRINCIPLES OF
EXISTENTIAL PRACTICE

In applying these philosophical principles to everyday life (and counselling and psy-
chotherapy are just specific examples of everyday life), the following practical princi-
ples emerge:

   There is a belief that not only is it possible to make sense of life, but also that it
     is good to try to make sense of life.
   Existentially we search for a model of living that can improve this particular per-
     son’s life, without endorsing any particular model. The guiding principle here is
     that the client should choose in the sense of owning and taking responsibility for
     whatever they select. Our task is to enable them to do this. Freedom without a
     sense of necessity, obligation, duty or responsibility does not exist.This applies just
     as much to what we aim for in our work with clients as it does to our relationship
     with them and also to how we live our own lives.
   We engage in a form of applied philosophy based upon the principles of
     phenomenology whose medium is dialogue and which requires commitment
     and full engagement for success. It consists of a careful and exhaustive description
     of experience leading to an understanding and verification of their implications
     and consequences.


                                         • 210 •
               • • •  Summing Up: Putting it All Together  • • •


   We aim for openness in understanding individual experience and a belief that
     personal problems can only be truly understood against the wider background of
     the physical, social, personal and spiritual dimensions of experience, and not
     purely as personal or intra-psychic issues.
   The search for well-being should be seen in relation to an understanding of the
     human condition, of life’s contradictions, tensions and dilemmas, and such prob-
     lems that arise are best seen as context-derived paradoxes and dilemmas and con-
     sequently can never be successfully solved by once-and-for-all either/or solutions.
     They can only be solved dialectically with a both/and solution and an understand-
     ing that any conclusion arrived at will only be provisional.
   Personal resilience comes from engagement with, not avoidance of, life’s contra-
     dictions, tensions and dilemmas.
   A successful outcome is not to make people happy or to enable them to live
     such that problems no longer exist but rather to enable people to have the
     courage to experience all the challenges and difficulties that a life in flux
     inevitably produces.



DEVELOPING A PERSONAL STYLE

We have seen in these pages that the existential approach is not only distinguished by
its philosophical clarity but also by its directness.While some specific interventions are
suggested in this book, the task for aspiring existential practitioners in work as in life
is to develop their own style, but one that is based firmly on the principles described
here. While other people may well inspire in different ways, no one will get very far
in life simply by copying other people. We live and die on our own account. Life is
the teacher as well as the judge, and existential interventions always come out of an
understanding of the human interaction between this particular client and this par-
ticular therapist at this particular time and place. We have made a careful distinction
between skills and techniques. We have described skills as intensely personal ways of
being that only become owned by perseverance and experience of failure, and have
spoken of techniques as mutually dehumanizing manualized strategies. Techniques on
their own are only appropriate if we see people as machines.
    What can be learnt in therapy, as in life, will always surprise us and it can never be
fully summarized in books or in prescriptive teachings. Human beings have to keep
searching to understand the realities of the human condition and of existence, rather
than taking it on authority from someone else without thinking.
    We are all responsible for our own point of view and our own learning but we
also have to acknowledge the autonomy of others. The difference between the
person we are and the job we do as therapists is only in the constraints of the set-
ting and the ethical boundaries. We are the sorts of therapist we are because of the
sort of person we are.
    Like life, this takes a lifetime to get right.

Existential philosophy, phenomenology and the practice of existential counselling
and psychotherapy tries to stay as close to lived human experience as possible. There
is a paradox here that was not lost on Picasso when he said, ‘We all know that Art
is not truth. Art is a lie that makes us realize truth’ (Fry, 1966: 165). Philosophy is
valuable but because it is abstract and aims to generalize, it involves a distancing from
everyday experience. Novels, narratives and fiction provide a greater possibility of
an experience-near description. The following list is of novels and films that contain
and expand existential themes in their different ways. This list is personal and idio-
syncratic, simply of works that resonate with us and that we believe will prompt
philosophical and personal reflection.

                                      • 218 •
       GLOSSARY OF
    EXISTENTIAL TERMS


Anxiety  is more than stress, depression or agitation. It is more like an apprehension:
a basic background unease, which nevertheless can come to the fore and sometimes
disables us in times of crisis. It involves a vague and discomforting awareness of the
unsolvable dilemmas of existence and usually includes a non-specific sense of danger,
a sense that we may die, that we are separate from other people, that we are respon-
sible for our own lives, and that we may fail to live up to our own moral code. This
specific form of anxiety is often termed ‘existential anxiety’ and it is assumed that
we cannot live without this form of essential discomfort, since it points us towards
our fundamental freedom and responsibility, without which we would not have
consciousness nor be human. In final analysis, anxiety is our life energy.
Assumptions  Human    beings are meaning-making creatures and cannot help but con-
struct meanings in terms of the values, beliefs and meanings they already have. These
meanings are simply assumptions that we have made in order to understand the world.
Such assumptions help us to make sense of things. By working phenomenologically
we can become more aware of the assumptions we habitually use to understand the
world and we can sharpen our own capacity to question them, reflect on them and
alter them as new information becomes available.
Authenticity/inauthenticity  does not refer to what is real or genuine but instead to our
ability to own and to be the author of our lives in full awareness that it will end and
that it is up to us to make something of it. There is no such thing as an authentic act.
Authenticity only arises when our attitude towards existence is one of facing truth
and when we allow this stance of truth seeking to inform our actions. As such it is
rarely sustainable and we inevitably come to deny our existential responsibility and so
revert back to inauthenticity, which is our more usual mode of being. When this in
turn becomes intolerable we may once again respond to the call of conscience and
own up to our responsibility. Authenticity and inauthenticity are two sides of the same
coin and are inseparable.
Autonomy  refers to the basic desire and ability of human beings to make their own
decisions about their lives and to be independent in their conduct and their thinking.
Such self-motivated behaviour is never an excuse for ignoring other people’s needs
and views.
      • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


Bad faith  (mauvaisefoi) is a term used by Sartre to refer to the way in which we
actively evade and deny our freedom and our nothingness. To be in bad faith is to
pretend to ourselves that we are either completely helpless and condemned to our fate
or completely free and able to be just anything we choose to be.
Change  is not something the existential therapist works to bring about. Since our
basic nature is one of permanent change, what we work to bring about is an under-
standing of how clients habitually resist, evade and deny change in their lives in order
to help them become aware of how they are avoiding making their own decisions
and choices.
Choice and responsibility  An   existential choice is about commitment to a course of
action and is not always simply about selection between alternatives. On many occa-
sions there is only one alternative on offer. It is about grasping life rather than evading
and denying life. Responsibility is a central theme of existential thinking. It refers to
an acknowledgement of personal accountability for one’s choices and actions. This
goes for those actions actively taken as well as for those which are attributed to our
context or to contingency. It is important to note that abstaining from choosing is also
a choice. The only choice we do not have is not to choose.
Crisis  A crisis is an opportunity and a moment when we realize not just the necessity
of action but when we become aware of the fragility of existence. We also become
aware that our future depends on what decision we make now. In a crisis we are called
to account for ourselves, and it is when we show our mettle. It is a time when we
realize that we can never guarantee with any certainty what will happen. Every
moment potentially has this quality but we habitually deny it, thinking that we are
passive recipients of our lives rather than active creators.
Death  marks  the end of our physical life and is the boundary that informs all our
decisions of how to live. Our life as a totality only becomes complete and therefore
fully meaningful on death. Living with awareness of the temporal nature of our
lives makes us aware of the urgency of living life to the full and makes it easier to
appreciate what is truly of value.
Defences  Not simply a psychological term, it refers to the way we remove from
awareness, principally by evasion and denial, the impact of the givens of existence that
threaten to expose us to ontological anxiety. Existential therapists will be inclined to
think more in terms of self-protection or avoidance of life, than in terms of defence
mechanisms as formulated by analysts.
Description/explanation  This is a crucial distinction in existential practice. Description
is central to phenomenology, which asks us to bracket off our assumptions about the
nature of something in favour of an experience-near description of what affects us.
Asking the client to describe will enable them to get closer to the truth of their expe-
rience and hence uncover its meaning. Explanation will, on the other hand, promote
a distancing and a more superficial understanding that will inevitably be in terms of
someone else’s understanding. Description reduces the influence of suggestion in
therapy whereas explanation increases it.


                                         • 220 •
                                  • • •  Glossary  • • •


Determinism  Human beings are undoubtedly moved and affected by numerous com-
plex forces, including forces of nature, such as gravity and entropy. We are biological
creatures shaped by our genes, but we are also shaped by social influences upon us, such
as those of education and economic factors. These dynamics affect our character, but
we are not entirely determined or caused by them. Human consciousness provides us
with the capacity to take a particular attitude towards these givens and go beyond them
in making sense of the world and also by affecting the way in which it will change in
future. We make something of what has been made of us. We transform our world.
Dialectic  isthe way dilemmas are solved, by bringing together two apparent opposites
to make something entirely new. It is not about compromise. Resolutions arrived at
dialectically are never fixed but permanently changing. Each new formulation con-
tains some of the former opposition whilst proposing a new idea. In dialectical living
we are prepared to continuously evolve and develop whilst learning from the past. In
the dialectic we hold the paradox and transcend it. We continuously evolve through
this dialectical process.
Dialogue  is  the essence of therapy, human interaction and the source of human mean-
ing. Buber says it is fleeting, challenging, difficult to achieve and sustain and can often
become two simultaneous monologues. Heidegger refers to the absence of dialogue
as ‘idle talk’. To be in dialogue is to be interactively involved in our intersubjectivity
and fully address each other and therefore come into our own and work our way
through whatever it is we are speaking about and engaging with.
Dilemma  All the important questions in life involve a dilemma, which cannot be
solved with a simple yes or no, right or wrong. For their meaning to be revealed the
tension within the dilemma needs to be engaged with resiliently. The existential
therapist’s job is to promote this greater engagement with the tensions of the dilemma.
This often involves making a person aware of the paradoxes of existence.
Embodiment  is the term given to the principle that the body is not something I have,
it is something I am. Perception, as Merleau-Ponty says, is only possible with and
through the body that is permanently attuned to the world it is a part of. To be fully
aware of something and experience and live it deeply and concretely rather than to
have opinions or theories about it is to become fully embodied.This is engaged living.
Empathy  in   the sense of being able to ‘stand in someone else’s shoes’ and feel what
they feel is an existential impossibility. What we call empathy is not a magical or tel-
epathic connection but an act of imagination borne out of our similarities as human
beings. Our ability to do this and to resonate with another person’s experience is
related to how well we have understood the lessons of life and how able we are to
comprehend the other’s experience.What is crucial for therapy and for life are not just
our similarities but also our differences, our ability to see things from different view-
points, to entertain alternative meanings. It is to be the same but also different.
Engagement  is to let the world and our own actions in it matter to us and to let our-
selves care about our life and actively connect with it. We can either try to detach
ourselves as much as possible and become withdrawn from our experience in order


                                         • 221 •
       • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


not to feel the hurt and difficulties that living involves, or we can be fully engaged and
passionately live our lives to the full no matter what.
Faith  is at the root of every meaningful action. We need to have a personal value sys-
tem that guides our actions and meanings. The paradox of faith is that we can never
be certain of anything but we have to act as if it is so, in full knowledge that it may
not be.This produces anxiety which we try to reduce by seeking certainty. Kierkegaard
spoke of taking a leap of faith and living like a knight of faith, which means to live in
a way that is fully engaged and where we do not try to exempt ourselves from life by
denial or special pleading.
Felt sense  isa term used by Gendlin to describe the essentially non- or pre-verbal
quality of a person’s bodily awareness. He regarded this somatic awareness as primary
and essentially is similar to what Heidegger called our ‘mood’, involving a complete
immersion in our world in a particular tonality. This includes sensation, emotion,
thought and intuition.
Four worlds, or dimensions  This   refers to the way we are not simply physical, social,
psychological or spiritual beings, but all four simultaneously. Each one of these ways
of being gives rise to an understanding or particular world or dimension of experi-
ence. We are inevitably engaged in the world at all these different levels at any time,
sometimes actively, sometimes passively, sometimes negatively by avoidance. We face
conflicts and contradictions at each level.
Freedom  is a core concept in all existential thinking. While we have a desire for a firm
and permanent moral code we know that one does not exist, and that we are therefore
free to make our own and are therefore also responsible for living up to it. As Sartre
says, ‘We are condemned to be free’, meaning that making something of our lives
within the constraints we all have is no one’s responsibility but our own. This also
means that we have to be prepared to think about an issue anew each time it presents
itself to us. To be free is not an option, but a given: human beings are fundamentally
free and capable of many different modes of being.
Givens  These    are the conditions of existence, each of which has an unsolvable
dilemma at its core. Also known as ‘ultimate concerns’, they each relate to one of the
four worlds: the physical, the social, the psychological and the spiritual. Some of the
inevitable givens of the human condition are that we are born to die, that we are
always with others and that we have to make our own decisions to the best of our
abilities. We have to work for our living and we will inevitably falter and fail; we shall
also be in doubt and feel guilt, know hardship, illness, doubt and despair. At the same
time it is a given that we have the capacity for consciousness, which allows us to rise
above all of these givens.
Guilt  inan existential sense refers not simply to having done something wrong in a
legal, common law or personal way. It refers to a sense of a debt or unease because of
having evaded or denied the givens of our existence or having betrayed our moral or
relational obligations to ourselves or others as fellow beings. The ultimate arbiter of
whether something is ‘wrong’ or not is not whether the culture deems it so but
whether it is coherent with the person’s value system, which has been developed in

                                        • 222 •
                                  • • •  Glossary  • • •


the light of the givens of existence. Existential guilt often refers specifically to us not
having done something we should or might have done, rather than to having done
something wrong. It is a feeling of being in debt to ourselves or to life.
Intentionality  is
                 a fundamental given of the nature of our consciousness: that we are
always conscious of something. There can be no human activity, physical, mental,
emotional or otherwise, without it having a certain direction, intention, object and
purpose. It is the nature of consciousness to go out of itself towards something. Often
it is enough to become aware of what our original intentions are if we want to live
more in tune with ourselves.
Knowing/unknowing, uncertainty/certainty  Some    things, few things, are knowable
and these are likely to be physical, factual and everyday things. When enquiring
into existence and meaning, however, we need to suspend – but not negate –
what we think we know. We temporarily become unknowing, which means that
we are open to surprise, to discovery, to something we did not expect. We are
once again naïve as children and full of wonder for what is. This is often experi-
enced as doubt or self-doubt, for in this process everything is in question. This
leads inevitably to the anxiety of uncertainty, which we may try to remove by
prematurely coming to certainty – persuading ourselves that we know, when we
do not. It may also lead to curiosity and more careful investigation. Sometimes,
when it is not yet possible to know something, this is where faith and the leap of
faith are important.
Leaping in/leaping ahead  Terms  used by Heidegger. Leaping in (or jumping in) refers
to when, usually out of their personal anxiety, a person takes over and suggests, directs
or advises the other in such a manner that another person’s autonomy is disabled.
Leaping ahead (or jumping ahead), by contrast, refers to an attitude of care for the
other, which acknowledges and respects their autonomy. In leaping ahead we are avail-
able and prepared for the other’s discovery of the road ahead with all its obstacles and
opportunities, without forcing their hand or making them decide which way to turn
at the crossroads.
Meaning and purpose/meaninglessness  The       desire and need for a personally defined
meaning and purpose to life is central to existential therapy as is the belief that a per-
son is able to define it for him or herself. The ability to define meaning and purpose
can only be resiliently arrived at when the person is able to confront the possibility
of meaninglessness.
Mineralization  is a term used by Sartre to describe how, when understanding the
world, we cannot help but make it seem fixed so that it seems as if set in stone. This
then hampers and restricts our freedom to see the world in any other way. This also
refers to Husserl’s term ‘sedimentation’, which is one of the ways in which mineraliza-
tion might occur.
Mood  We are always in some mood and our mood is not passive or irrelevant. It may
seem so constant, familiar and habitual that we do not question it. But it is active and
chosen and indicates how individually we make the world matter and how we are
pervasively immersed in world in a certain way.

                                         • 223 •
       • • •  Skills in Existential Counselling & Psychotherapy  • • •


Ontological/ontic  A distinction made by Heidegger and other philosophers to refer to
the difference and relationship between, on the one hand, the essential, necessary and
universally valid conditions of human existence – the ontological – and on the other
hand, the concrete, changing and practical aspects of existence – the ontic. Existential
therapists endeavour to bring clients back to the root of their everyday (ontic) prob-
lems so they can be seen in the light of the inevitable (ontological) givens of existence.
For example, ontological anxiety arising from the inevitability of death and ageing
may lead one person to ontically attempt to deny ageing and hence death by cosmetic
surgery, another to desire immortality through fame and a third to want to commit
suicide so as to escape from the perceived threat.
Paradox  Human     existence is paradoxical in that, for example, we can only come truly
to life by facing up to the reality of the inevitability of death. We can only be close to
others if we have become capable of being strong in our loneliness. Paradoxes are part
of life and the existential therapist does not aim to eliminate them but to live con-
structively with their tension. Indeed, it is this tension that creates the force of life.
Possibility/Potentiality  Thisdoes not refer just to what might happen in an everyday
sense but to the essential freedom of humankind to choose our actions and live with
the consequences. It is often referred to as ‘potentiality’ and is an ontological quality of
Being. Heidegger claimed that potentiality was always greater than actuality. Existentially,
as we are perpetually in a state of becoming, we never achieve our full potential.
Relationship/Relationality  Existential thinking rejects the notion of the individual
person who has relationships with others and with the world, for the idea that person
is no more and no less than their relationships. Human beings are essentially relational.
We cannot be without a world, without others and without consciousness, which is
essentially relational in nature. This goes deeper than simply needing relationships in
a social sense. The person does not exist separately from their relationships. We are
connective with the world, we are not separate from it, but intertwined with it. As
with the body, a person does not have relationships, they are their relationships. With
respect to therapy, the nature and quality of the relationship determines how well the
therapy progresses. It is the ways in which the therapist and client strive to have a true
dialogue and together overcome the obstacles they face that constitutes the work of
therapy.
Sedimentation  Similar  to mineralization but is a term used by Husserl and then
Merleau-Ponty to refer to the notion that we often believe or act as if truth is fixed,
and therefore that our lives and identities are fixed. In actual fact nothing is fixed but
our ideas and conceptions of reality. They form through experience and can then
become like sediments that settle in the river of life, creating obstacles in the flow, but
also footholds and stepping stones when they become mineralized (see: mineralization).
Self  Existential authors reject the idea of the self as something which is fixed in favour
of the self as a process. It makes more sense to change the noun ‘the self ’ into a verb
‘to selve’. The self is always in flux, always in process of becoming and always in rela-
tion. In this sense the statement ‘I am an angry sort of person’ says, for example, more
about the way the speaker restricts their possibility than it says about their ‘true’ nature.

                                         • 224 •
                                  • • •  Glossary  • • •


Situatedness  Although, as Sartre says, we are condemned to be free, our freedom
always takes place in a particular context. It is always situated, and this is what makes
our freedom meaningful in the sense that potential can be real-ized (i.e. made real).
Human existence can never be abstracted from its world because we are always in-
the-world.
Thrownness and fallenness  Thrownness refers to the existential fact that we are born
into this world with no say about where or when or even if we come to life. Our first
task therefore is to acknowledge this, to choose it in the sense of owning it. Our life
starts from a position of randomness and chance. Having owned our thrownness, the
next task is to make something of the world we find ourselves in. By constantly looking
to others for a lead we fall out of touch with our autonomy and our authentic potential.
We often confuse thrownness with fallenness in an attempt to avoid responsibility.
Time and temporality  Time    is one of the boundaries to human existence – the way
we live our lives is defined by how we see death and the passing of all things, includ-
ing ourselves. Temporality is the quality of living in time. We do not have or use or
waste time, we are time. Time is manifest in us, for time is transformation and human
beings are the instruments and the place of change.
Truth  isusually defined as what is factually correct or real. Existential thinkers are con-
cerned with truth as a value. This is not just a subjective evaluation of correctness – ‘It
feels right so it must be true’ – but an evaluation in accordance with the givens, dilem-
mas and paradoxes of existence. To arrive at truth requires a dialectical combination of
subjective, objective and existential factors. It is to be expected that we can rarely know
the entire and total truth of anything, but this does not mean that such truth does not
exist or that we should not strive for it.
Understanding  is an essential value in existential therapy and can be contrasted with
the idea of knowledge. Understanding is about an embodied, engaged awareness of
something in a meaningful way, rather than in terms of purely being able to quote
the facts and figures of something. It is about comprehension rather than about
explanation.
Values and beliefs  are  central to meaning. Psychotherapy is not amoral, not value-free,
and encouraging a client to examine their values and beliefs in order that they better
understand their implications will enable a client to more clearly establish and there-
fore choose which values to live by and which possibilities to actualize. While exis-
tential therapists start by challenging established and frozen frameworks of norms and
standards, they embark on a search for the values that are in tune with what is and that
work for individuals and the wider community.
Worldview  refers to the sense we make of the world we are a part of. There is a para-
dox in that it provides us with structure, without which we might despair. This
explains why we get so attached to our viewpoint. But it also prevents us from con-
sidering alternatives and to continue our search for truth. A worldview can be made
explicit and questioned or transcended; it can also be turned into a dogma and a
prescriptive ideology. Existential therapists have to guard against these dangers like
everyone else.
